    **THE LIMIT, measured, and NOT a print defect: the reader paginates the reference report to 8 pages where Word makes 19.** Ruled out by measurement, so nobody re-derives them: content is complete (7,314 characters in `word/document.xml` against 7,321 in the storage — an earlier "193,094" was a regex that let `<w:t[^>]*>` match `<w:tbl>`), the line count is right (689 line fragments), the 18pt `w:docGrid` line pitch is parsed and applied, images are not it (2 attachments, 202pt, none zero-height), deferred giant tables are not it (storage identical after the splice), and explicit page breaks are not it (ONE `w:br w:type="page"`, zero `w:pageBreakBefore` in the document AND in `styles.xml`). What remains is concentrated in Word's own front matter — its pages 1–6 hold 51 lines between them — plus tables this reader cannot split across a page (invariant 55/58's known edge), which is why 3 of our 8 printed pages carry table rows inside the margins. A paged-fidelity item (`docs/06-research/2026-07-29-paged-fidelity/`), not something printing can fix.

60. **The View menu's three page toggles — page outline / header / footer — are a LAYOUT switch, not a visibility flag, and the sheets they draw are the same rectangles printing puts on paper.** `PageViewOptions` (global, in `UserDefaults`, all three ON by default) decides three things at once: which entries `MarkdownDocument.applyPageBand` passes on (a hidden header is passed as NO ENTRIES, so it is neither measured nor painted — one mechanism, not a flag each consumer re-checks), whether the band exists at all with nothing drawn in it (`separatesPages`), and whether the page-break hairline is drawn (`drawsDivider` — a real sheet edge says it better, so the rule yields to the outline). `applyPageBand` is called from BOTH the render and `reapplyPageBand`, deliberately one function: a toggle re-deriving the band with its own copy of that arithmetic would drift from the render's the first time either was touched.

    **(a) "쭉 연결되어 보이고" means the band must not be RESERVED.** The comments panel can set a bool and repaint (invariant 38) because its marks sit on glyphs that do not move. This cannot — so a toggle re-solves the band and re-lays out, in invariant 56's order: scroll to the top FIRST (invalidating with the clip parked deep makes the next draw fill every hole in one call — 87,638 ms against 937.9), then invalidate, then restore the reading position from the layout walk's completion, never before it. It must never reach `MarkdownDocument.render`: nothing is rebuilt, `renderGeneration` does not move, and the reading column of a paged document never shifts by a point.

    **(b) The restore is PARKED, not a completion closure — `precomputeLayout` cancels an in-flight walk whenever a new one starts (`layoutToken`), and a cancelled walk's completion never runs.** Measured while building this: a toggle whose walk was superseded by the render's own opening tail silently lost the reader's place, landing them at character 0 from 75% down. `pendingAnchor` is applied by whichever walk reaches the end, so supersession becomes a later restore instead of a lost one.

    **(c) The desk between two sheets is `RenderTheme.pageDeskGap`, the one number here no document declares.** Two stacked sheets of paper TOUCH, so the space between them on screen is a property of the desk. This is NOT invariant 57(e)'s invented constant returning: that one claimed to be the document's own inter-page space, which the document HAD stated. Reserved only while the outline is on, never printed — `PagePagination.sheets` takes it back off, so the paper is exactly the document's own sheet and `makePrintOperation` reads its height from the sheet rather than the pitch. **Getting this wrong looked like nothing had happened:** the first version had no desk, the sheets tiled edge to edge, the desk fill was covered by the very sheets it sat behind, and the feature drew as a hairline indistinguishable from the page-break rule it replaces. Confirmed by pixel-sampling the capture — gap and paper both `(255,254,255)`.

    **(d) With the outline on, the FIRST and LAST pages get their full margins** (`leading = max(headerLeading, marginTop)`, and the mirror for the footer). Otherwise page 1's sheet begins one top margin above the first line, which with only a header's worth of leading room is ABOVE the view — so page 1 draws with no top border and a visibly shorter margin than every page after it. The two outer PAINT arms then place their content at the document's own header/footer distance INTO that room, reducing to the previous `top: 0` whenever the reservation is only as tall as the header.

    **(e) Owner decisions, settled here rather than asked.** GLOBAL rather than per window (a reader who wants continuous flow wants it for every document, and the choice must survive a relaunch); all three ON by default (the owner's framing was *"페이지 외곽 모양(그럼 선이 필요 없어짐)"* — the outline REPLACES the rule, so shipping it off would hide the feature); NO drop shadow (it costs a blur per sheet on every scroll pass, and the desk plus a hairline already reads as paper). One menu click to change.

    **(f) A page is CENTRED on the paper's width, and four separate things kept undoing that** — all four reported by the owner looking at the screen, each a different mechanism. (1) `sizeToFit` widens a text view's frame back to its clip view, so `PageCenteringClipView`'s `frame < clip` test silently stopped firing — measured at 727.2pt of frame around a 595.3pt sheet, and 909.0pt after a zoom press, which also drove the magnification to `clipFrame ÷ frame` = 0.8876 (a zoom-IN that zoomed OUT, two feedback loops solving for each other). Fixed at the root: the clip view reads `ReaderTextView.pagedPaperWidth`, a constant of the DOCUMENT, so no frame drift can reach it; the frame is additionally pinned by `pinPagedFrameWidth` after every `sizeToFit` and every magnification change, because the frame is the SCROLLABLE area and a wrong one lets a reader scroll into empty desk. (2) AppKit calls `constrainBoundsRect` when the bounds ORIGIN changes, not when the clip's FRAME does — so opening the table of contents narrowed the reading area and left the old origin; `recentrePage()` now asks for the constraint explicitly. (3) THREE scroll call sites hard-coded `x: 0` (`restore`, `scrollCharToTop`, and the reveal in the outline jump); each meant "scroll vertically to here" and each undid the centring on every reflow, which is why the page snapped left on opening AND closing the panel. They now preserve `clip.bounds.origin.x`. (4) The desk BESIDE the page is the scroll view's background (`syncDeskBackground`) — the text view cannot paint outside its own bounds, so without it a centred page sat on the same white as the paper and read as no page at all.

    **(f2) ONE function applies the whole paged view state — the fix for the CLASS rather than for those four bugs.** Five rules describe a paged view (which width is the paper, how wide the scrollable frame is, what colour the space beside the page is, whether the page is centred, whether a sideways scroller is needed) and they were applied by FIVE paths each handling a different subset. Audited: the reading-column settle did all five, a magnification change three, `sizeToFit` one, and the View-menu page toggle **none** — which is why switching the outline off left the desk colour behind. `applyPagedViewState()` does all five and every path calls only that; it is a no-op for a document with no page, so the non-paged branch goes through the SAME function rather than a second divergent one. Two more single-source repairs from the same audit: `pageSheets` is DERIVED from `printSheets` (two copies of the sheet arithmetic is how screen and paper would come to disagree), and the desk gap is carried on `PageBandLayoutDelegate.deskGap` — the layout that RESERVED it — rather than re-read from the preference at draw time, which left a window where a paint would take the new answer against the old band and mis-tile every sheet by 12pt. **Testing this needs care:** a toggle's own layout walk ends in `sizeTextViewToFit`, which applies the same state, so a test that waits for the async tail passes whether or not the TOGGLE did it — fake-green, confirmed by mutation. Assert IMMEDIATELY after the toggle, which is also what a reader sees.

    **(g) A panel that takes width away shrinks the page to fit; nothing ever enlarges it.** `shrinkPageZoomToFit` is one-directional by instruction — *"무리하게 줄이진 말고, 창이 우측으로 삐져나가지 않도록 축소해서 맞추라는 뜻"* — so the sidebar and the comments inspector call it and a manual window resize just reveals more desk (the removed "zoom follows the window" rule stays removed). It fits `paper × zoom` into the reading area MINUS `pageWindowSideMargin` on each side.

    **(h) A page break is never drawn through a table.** A line inside a table cannot be shifted (invariant 55/58), so a table crossing a boundary overruns it and layout opens no band — and a sheet drawn on the arithmetic grid put its edge, and the desk behind it, in the middle of the table's rows. `PagePagination.joiningUnopenedBoundaries` unions those sheets, which says the true thing: this page ran longer than its paper. SCREEN ONLY — paper cannot stretch, so `rectForPage` keeps the paper-sized grid, and that divergence is the medium's rather than two implementations disagreeing.

    **(i) A stored global preference makes any test that measures the band depend on it.** `PageBandReservationTests` pins the pre-feature shape in `setUp` (outline off, header and footer on) — otherwise nine of its assertions move with the shipped default and the suite reads differently for a developer who had switched the outline off. Every new test here resets the store on both sides.

61. **A table that will not fit on the rest of its page is MOVED WHOLE to the next one — decided by measuring a layout that already happened, and carried out by shifting exactly ONE line.** Until this existed a table ran past its page's text bottom, so its rows printed inside the margin: on the reference report 4 of 16 tables overran by 71 / 139 / 145 / 236pt, three of its eight sheets. The screen could tell that truth (`PagePagination.joiningUnopenedBoundaries` unions the sheets), paper cannot.


    **THE OWNER'S POLICY, stated here so it is not found six sub-items down — measurements and the three parts are in (f).**

    > 셀이 페이지를 넘어가면 **잘라서** 표현한다. 그런데 윗부분이 **2~3줄 차이로 애매하면** 그냥 셀을 **다음 페이지부터** 시작하게 넘긴다.

    Given twice in the same words — *"3줄 이하일 때만 다음 페이지로 넘겨야지 왜 통째로 넘겨"* and the line above. The threshold lives in ONE place, `DocumentWindowController.orphanRunStarts` (`lines.count <= 3`), and the record it fills is `PageBandLayoutDelegate.pullToNextPage`. Anything that reads "a table line cannot be shifted" describes a MECHANISM, never this policy: the policy is that such a table IS broken, and only a stub of three lines or fewer is carried on.

    **(a) SPLITTING was built and measured here, and invariant 64 later shipped it under a menu — read both.** What follows is why it is not the DEFAULT and can never be unconditional. Letting the ordinary between-page rule shift table lines produces a real Word-like split, and on a table of single-line rows it is flawless: rows stay whole, each page's segment keeps its own border box, header and footer land in the gap (`docs/fixtures/office/paged-visual/tablepage.docx`, captured). On a real report it TEARS. A vertically merged cell spans the boundary, so the half of the row that moved leaves that cell stretched across the gap — with this reader's own running header and page number painted inside the table — and the row's two halves end up a page apart. Splitting honestly needs `NSTextTableBlock` geometry we deliberately do not own (invariants 39/42). The safe-row-boundary variant is what invariant 64 built; it does not save the reference report's first table, whose left column is merged across the very boundary in question, so that one is still carried whole.

    **(b) ONE line is enough, measured not assumed.** The typesetter positions each line after the previous one, so shifting a table's first line carries every later line with it — the property the between-page rule already relies on. Pushing the reference report's table #0 left its height identical (438.45pt both ways) and landed its visual top exactly on target. **But the first line in TEXT order is not the table's top**: a vertically merged cell is centred in its own span, measured 21.35pt below the table's top edge on that same table, so `TableMetrics.topInset` carries that distance. Moving by the naive `target - rect.minY` would land the visible top that far into the page.

    **(c) MEASURE, then place — the height does not exist when the typesetter asks.** At the moment `shouldSetLineFragmentRect` is called for a table's first line nothing has measured that table, so `DocumentWindowController.settlePagedTables` walks the COMPLETED layout, decides (`PagePagination.tablesToPush`), and re-solves. That extra pass rides `precomputeLayout`'s existing chunked walk, so no single turn grows. Cost on real documents (10k–27k characters, 8–13 tables moved): **2–3 rounds, 85–260ms total**, of which the measuring step is **1–4ms** — the cost is entirely the re-layouts. A document with nothing to move pays one line-fragment walk and stops.

    **(d) The two refusals are load-bearing.** A table TALLER than the page is never moved: it would overrun the next page just as far, having emptied the one it left (a 660pt table on a 220pt page — moving it adds a near-empty page and changes nothing else). And an already-decided entry is kept VERBATIM rather than re-derived: a moved table now fits, so re-deriving would drop it, which would move it back, which would make it not fit — the settle would never converge. Because the record only grows and the layout rule is idempotent (a table sitting at a page top declines to move again), the loop terminates on its own; `maxPagedTableSettles` is a backstop, not the mechanism.

    **(e) The whole document is invalidated each round, deliberately.** Moving a table changes which boundaries LAYOUT opened, and `openedBoundaries` is what the painter and the page outline read — keeping the earlier half of that record while re-deriving the rest would leave the two halves describing different layouts. Re-deriving all of it is one honest pass and (c) says it is affordable.

    **(f) Printing settles SYNCHRONOUSLY first.** ⌘P straight after opening is exactly when the asynchronous settle has not finished, and paper has no honest way to show an overrun. `makePrintOperation` calls `settlePagedTablesFully` before it decides anything.

    **(g) The async seam needs its own test, and it must wait on the RESULT.** Every other check in `PagedTableOverrunTests` calls the settle itself, which proves the rule and says nothing about whether opening a document REACHES it (invariant 29) — a version whose walk never settled would leave them all green while every reader still printed rows in the margin. That test cannot use a completion callback (a cancelled walk's completion never runs — invariant 60b), and it cannot stop at the first sign of life: one round only moves what overran where it stood, and moving it makes later tables overrun, so a wait that stopped there caught the document mid-repagination with three tables still in a margin. It polls until no table ends in a margin.

    **(h) The settle reaches a DIFFERENT fixed point depending on how much asynchronous work ran first, so the reader and `--pdf` can disagree about the page count — measured, and NOT a content loss.** On `testdocs/everything/1790387_prep_final_report.hwpx`: `--pdf` produces **147 pages** (98 tables pushed, 143 boundaries opened, 32 lines in a margin) while the same document printed from an in-process controller produces **156** (92 pushed, 151 opened, 64 in a margin) — with **identical options** (`outline` on, `splitTables` off), identical band (159.37), pitch (853.88), page body (694.51), column (481.9) and window (820×640), and **identical content: 5,492 laid-out lines both ways**. Nothing is missing from the shorter PDF; the same lines are packed differently, and the headless one is the CLEANER by the margin metric. Settling FROM SCRATCH is stable within a process (`pushedTables` cleared + full invalidate + `settlePagedTablesFully`, three rounds: 92 / 156 / `used=133031.36` every time, to the hundredth), so invariant 61(d)'s convergence fear is not what makes these disagree — the starting layout is. Anyone fixing this must pin WHERE the settle starts, not make it re-derive.

    **(i) The residual margin overruns are tables the settle DECLINED to register, and `pushWholeTable` is structurally unable to help them.** It moves a table only when the settle put it in `pushedTables` (`guard let metrics = pushedTables[charRange.location]`), so every other table line is refused — traced on that document: **1,550 refusals**, of which the ones surviving to the final layout are the 32. Turning "Split Tables Across Pages" ON changes **nothing** on this file (147 pages, the same 29 overrun pages, the same three severe ones at +76.5/+68.0/+76.4pt), which is invariant 64(a)'s point measured a second way. And the overrun is not always where it looks: the visible page-34 break sits beside ordinary paragraphs (checked in the source XML — no enclosing `hp:tbl`), while the table that actually overruns is a neighbouring 2-row one. Judge this class from the delegate's own refusals, never from the rendered page.

    **Numbers, and one honest limit.** Reference report 4 overruns → **0**, 8 tables moved, **8 → 11 pages**; its ODT twin 1 → 0; `tago-tables.odt` 5 → 0. `--extract` byte-identical (13,914 bytes — this lives in the RENDER layer, invariant 40), and a document with no page width is never touched (invariant 57d). The page count moving toward Word's 19 is a coincidence of direction, NOT convergence: Word SPLITS tables by default (`w:cantSplit` absent), so a reader that moves them will never reproduce Word's pagination. That gap stays a paged-fidelity item, not a printing defect.

62. **A header or footer that BUILDS to nothing must reserve nothing — "the entry has blocks" is the wrong question, and 28% of real Korean documents answer it wrongly.** `w:titlePg`'s deliberately blank cover header arrives with NO blocks and was always handled; an entry made of nothing but EMPTY PARAGRAPHS arrives with blocks, measured a full line height, and opened a band on every page with nothing in it. Measured over 548 real `.hwp`/`.hwpx`: 94 declare a header or footer at all, and **26 of those 94 (28%) declare one that draws nothing** — and the reference report's own `.odt` declares exactly such a first-page header, which is why it reserved 42.55pt above its first line for a blank. `PageBandGeometry.drawsSomething` decides it from what the entry BUILDS, and `entryDraws` is the single predicate both the band measurement and `DocumentWindowController.configurePageBand`'s leading-band gate ask (they had drifted: fixing only the measurement left the leading gate still asking `blocks.isEmpty`). Two things it must keep allowing: a picture-only header (an attachment is `U+FFFC`, not whitespace, so the text test passes it) and a header drawing only a RULE or shaded band (no glyph reaches the built string, so the BLOCKS are asked for `shading`/`borderEdges` directly). Invariant 47's three-state lesson one level deeper: present, present-but-empty, absent. Found by running `FMD_HEADER_FOOTER_PROBE` across real HWP — which also **closed the "HWP printing unverified" gap**: `.hwp`, `.hwpx`, `.odt` and `.docx` now all pass it, header and footer built to real glyphs with live page numbers and a PDF whose page count matches the reader's (140 / 45 / 450 / 8 pages). The probe itself had to be corrected twice: it pinned no page options, so invariant 60(d)'s full-margin first page read as a failure, and it asked `blocks.isEmpty` too.

63. **`NSWindowController.document` is `unowned(unsafe)`, and this reader asks for it FROM THE DRAW PATH — so every read goes through `DocumentWindowController.mdDocument`, never `document as? MarkdownDocument`.** A window can outlive its document by a run-loop turn, and CoreAnimation commits the next draw inside that turn: `ReaderTextView.drawBackground` → `drawPageSheets` → `pageSheets` → `printSheets` → `pagedDocumentWidth` → `pagedWidth` → `objc_retain` on freed memory. The cast does it — an `unowned(unsafe)` read producing a `+1` reference walks the dead object. It killed the WHOLE SUITE with SIGSEGV, deterministically, and two crash reports a day apart show that identical frame; what made it deterministic was a new test that pumps the run loop. A `weak` mirror kept in step by overriding the setter answers `nil` instead — the same answer every non-paged document already gives, so no caller changed. **Note `swift test` HIDES this**: it reports the suite as merely "failed" with truncated output and no crash report. Run the bundle directly — `swift build --build-tests` then `$DEVELOPER_DIR/usr/bin/xctest .build/arm64-apple-macosx/debug/FastDocReaderPackageTests.xctest`, which exits 139. **There is deliberately no unit test** (invariant 56's reason — every shape tried was fake-green: nil-ing the local reference does not release the document because the window keeps it alive, and `removeWindowController` clears the controller's own pointer so the UNFIXED code answers nil too); verified by that mutation, and the gap is written into `PagedZoomTests`.

64. **A table that will not finish on its page is either BROKEN at a row boundary or carried whole — the reader's choice, except that a table taller than the page is always broken.** The View menu's "Split Tables Across Pages" (`PageViewOptions.splitTables`, global, OFF by default) picks between them; the owner's rule *"표가 한장이 넘을때... 이런 경우는 무조건 쪼개야 해"* overrides it, because a table with no whole page to be carried to could only ever be left with rows in a margin.

    **(a) THE PIECE THAT MOVES IS NOT A ROW — it is the run of rows no merged cell spans.** Breaking above row `R` is safe only when no block has `startingRow < R < startingRow + rowSpan`; break where one does and the merged cell stretches across the gap between two pages with this reader's own header painted inside it, and the row's halves end up a page apart (rendered and looked at — invariant 61a). Rows welded by a merge travel together (`PagePagination.unbreakableGroups`). Registering plain ROWS instead was built and measured on the reference report, which is merged nearly everywhere: the safe rows moved, the merged stretches between them did not, and twenty lines still landed in margins. A table nothing can be broken inside falls back to being carried whole even with breaking ON — better whole on the next page than half in a margin.

    **(b) Both answers are correct pagination, and neither is Word's.** Measured: reference report 11 pages carried / 10 broken, its `.odt` 8 / 7, a 39-page HWP 39 / 38, a 483-page HWPX 496 / 483 — and ZERO rows in a margin either way on every in-repo fixture. Word splits by default (`w:cantSplit` absent), so its page count will not be reproduced by carrying; a paged-fidelity item (invariant 59).

    **(c) The settle re-measures every round, and the metrics are ROUNDED to a hundredth of a point so it can stop.** A piece's height comes back as `161.70000000000002` one round and `161.7` the next, and the loop compares the whole record — without rounding it would never converge. Measured convergence: 2–4 rounds, 95–427ms, on documents from 7k to 100k characters.

    **(d) The page a point sits on needs a hair of TOLERANCE, and this is not tidiness.** A piece moved to a page top lands at exactly `page × pitch + leadingBand`, and that number does not survive the division — measured, a line placed at page 6's top came back as `5051.279999999` against a pitch product of `5051.28`, so a bare `floor` called it page 5, judged it against page 5's bottom, and reported a piece that had just been moved correctly as still overrunning. `PageBandLayoutDelegate.page(of:leadingBand:pitch:)` owns the tolerance and `PagePagination`'s own check uses the same one — the two must agree exactly or the decision keeps asking for a move the rule has already made.

    **(e) THE LEDGER WAS UNDER-MEASURING, and that half IS fixed.** A row's extent was taken from its LINE FRAGMENTS, and a cell draws its padding and border OUTSIDE its glyphs — measured at **3.48pt per cell** over 2,674 cells of a real report, i.e. ~70pt across a twenty-row group, exactly the gap that let a piece the arithmetic said would fit still end in a margin. `DocumentWindowController.laidOutTables` now grows every line rect by its own block's `width(for: .padding/.border, edge: .minY/.maxY)`, applied to every line rather than only the first and last (an interior line's inset loses to the `min`/`max` anyway). Paired A/B on one build: 편람 **580 → 566** lines in a margin with breaking off and **599 → 592** with it on, its `.hwpx` twin **35 → 33** and 36 → 36 with the worst overrun falling 174.37 → 164.13pt. Judged by `topInset`, which is 0 for a ledger built from glyphs alone and is that cell's own top padding once right (mutation-checked).

    **(f) THE LIMIT, measured and NOT closed.** On very large real Korean documents rows still land in margins: 205 lines of a 38-page HWP and 771 of a 483-page HWPX. **192 and 556 of those are inside a single unbreakable piece TALLER THAN THE PAGE** — a one-row table used as a text box, holding a page and more of prose — which cannot be placed anywhere without breaking a row, i.e. splitting one cell's text across two pages, the tear this design exists to avoid. That part is honest. The REST (13 and 215 lines) is unexplained and should not be re-derived blind: the settle converges (4 rounds, and raising the cap from 8 to 40 changes nothing), the delegate SEES every registered piece (1040 of 1040), and it declines them because the recorded height says they fit — traced on one, `visualTop 24446.62 + height 161.70 = 24608.32` against a page bottom of `24636.04`, while that piece's own lines reach `24682`. So a piece's measured extent is smaller than what is drawn inside it, by about its own `topInset`; the row bookkeeping in `DocumentWindowController.laidOutTables` (which keys a merged cell's whole extent to its STARTING row) is the place to look. In-repo fixtures show zero, which is why this needs a real document. **And breaking makes that residue WORSE, the opposite of what the toggle promises** — measured twice on the two documents the owner uses as the release gate: `2025_행정업무운영편람_최종.hwp` **723 → 807** lines in a margin (off → on) and `1790387_prep_final_report.hwpx` **23 → 30**, while the WORST overrun is identical to the hundredth either way (354.68pt / ~175pt), i.e. the dominant offender is the same over-tall unbreakable piece in both configurations and breaking merely adds new boundaries that overrun. The toggle is not a repair for this residue and must not be recommended as one; it stays OFF by default, which these numbers say is better on real Korean documents.

65. **Decoded pixels outlive the document that needed them, and that is what "the app never gives memory back" actually was.** Measured on the release build: freshly launched **15 MB**; open a 20 MB HWPX → 161 MB; open a 10.7 MB HWP and a 14.4 MB DOCX after it, close every window, and it sat at **224 MB with nothing open** — which is why an instance left running 44 hours held **471 MB** with a single markdown file on screen. It reads as a leak and is not one: `leaks` reported **287 leaks / 14 KB**, while `heap` found **1,374 live 160 KB blocks** — decoded images, still legitimately referenced from `MarkdownDocument.imageCache`/`officeImageCache`, keyed per document (path + id, so nothing collides) but scoped to the PROCESS. `purgeImageCaches()` drops them when the LAST document closes and returns 34–97 MB per close; while anything is open they stay, because that is what makes scrolling back to a picture instant, and `NSCache` already evicts under real pressure. **Three things this cost.** (a) The hook is `MarkdownDocument.close()` AFTER `super.close()`, deferred one run-loop turn — read the count at window-close time and the closing document is still registered, so it never fires. (b) `malloc_zone_pressure_relief` was built and REMOVED: measured in BOTH orders — before the purge it returned 0 MB immediately and 0 MB three seconds later (correct: the memory was still referenced), and after the purge, on a close that demonstrably gave back 97 MB, it still returned 0 MB. (c) The first diagnosis was WRONG and stood on one sample: an early `heap` run reporting 16.6 MB live was read as "nothing is retained, the allocator is just holding pages", and the next run on the same shape said 264 MB. One sample of a process's heap is not a measurement. What does NOT come back is ~110 MB of AppKit/CoreText caches, not ours to drop.

66. **Paper has no viewport — printing must load every picture first, and only the count of them proves it.** Pixels are held for a window either side of the reader (invariant 1), right for reading and silently wrong for printing: a 14.4 MB report carrying 28 PNGs, printed straight after opening, produced a 50-page PDF holding **ONE image object** and weighing 1.9 MB — every other picture came out as blank reserved space with nothing to say so, true of ⌘P as much as of the CLI. `makePrintOperation` now asks for the whole document (`reconcileMedia(in:loadingEverything:)`), fixing both paths at once because both come through it: 43 image objects and 14.1 MB, about a second added. The load there is SYNCHRONOUS (`loadOfficeImageSync`) and that is load-bearing — the print operation is built and run on the same turn, so the ordinary callback lands after the PDF has been written; the first fix used the async path and changed nothing, which is exactly what a passing-looking non-fix looks like. `--pdf` introduces no pagination: it builds a real off-screen window controller, settles the same asynchronous pipeline, and hands the SAME operation ⌘P builds to a `.save` disposition — verified on `.docx` (11 pages), `.hwp` (490), `.odt` (9) and `.md` (423). Sandboxing note, **superseded by invariant 70 — read that one, not this sentence**: it used to say the App Store build cannot write a PDF at all, so `--pdf` belonged to the direct build and must not be advertised in store copy. That is now wrong in both halves. The sandboxed build writes into any folder the user has GRANTED (measured again 2026-08-07 by `Scripts/smoke-headless-pdf.sh` against a `SANDBOX=1` release: 308 pages into `~/Documents`, and an ungranted destination refused in 2.0 s with the hint that says what would fix it). The honest store claim is "works in folders you have granted", never "does not work". What remains true is only that it cannot write to an ARBITRARY path with no grant behind it.

67. **A Swift value carried as a text attribute must be `Hashable`, not merely `Equatable`.** `NSMutableAttributedString` hashes attribute values when deciding whether two adjacent runs can coalesce, so an `Equatable`-only Swift struct makes the runtime print "Obj-C `-hash` invoked on a Swift value of type … that is Equatable but not Hashable; this can lead to severe performance problems" — which this app did on every office document, for `OfficeGraphicInfo` and `FillMarginTabInfo` (and therefore `TabStop`/`TabAlignment`/`TabLeader`). Adding the conformance is free and mechanical. **Measuring it is not, and the method is the part worth keeping:** rebuilding twice and comparing wall clocks was useless, because this machine's load moved the same measurement by 3× between arms (load 7.8 against 26.9 reversed the answer). `AttributeHashingSpikeTests` declares both shapes in ONE file and times them back to back in a single process, alternating, so whatever the machine is doing is done to both — **8.1 ms against 6.0 ms over 4,000 attribute runs**. The gain is real and modest; the spike asserts only the direction, never a millisecond figure.

68. **ONE binary serves three shapes — reader, headless flags, and the Finder's preview — and the branch that separates the third is the FIRST line of `main.swift`.** `Contents/PlugIns/QuickLookPreview.appex` holds a COPY of the app's executable, named `QuickLookPreview` after its own bundle rather than after the app it was copied from (the store rejects any other name, 90362 — and the name is free to differ because nothing routes on it); `Bundle.main.bundlePath.hasSuffix(".appex")` routes it to `NSExtensionMain` and never returns, so the preview shares 100% of the reader with **no module split** — the whole point, because splitting the package into a library would mean annotating hundreds of symbols and touching all 1,366 tests, and a preview-only renderer would make "the preview IS the reader" a lie (alternatives and costs in `docs/02-planned/quick-look-extension-design.md`). **`NSExtensionMain` is a `main` and really reads `argc`/`argv`**: declared with no parameters it inherits whatever is in those registers and dies in `strlen` inside `EXExtensionMain` (`EXC_BAD_ACCESS`, read off the crash report) — and the only symptom from outside is "the extension registers but no preview appears", which looks like a packaging problem. Measured format by format by watching WHICH extension process runs: `.md`, `.hwp` and our own config types come to us — Mocha Drop's competing markdown/HWP extension included — while `.docx`/`.odt` stay with Apple's built-in. `QLSupportedContentTypes` deliberately omits `public.plain-text`/csv/tsv/log: macOS previews those fine and claiming them would take over every plain-text preview for nothing. It DOES claim `public.toml` and `com.microsoft.ini`, the other half of invariant 69(a): our own exported `config-text` covers `.conf`/`.env`/`.vars`, but `.cfg` really resolves to `public.toml` and `.ini` to `com.microsoft.ini` — types someone else owns, so no tag of ours reaches them and those two previewed as somebody else's while the app opened them. Claiming the owner's type is the only route, and it takes `.toml` along with `.cfg`; accepted rather than accidental, and consistent (the app opens `.toml` too). Cost, release: the app goes **17 MB → 27 MB**, because the copy is a copy (the size card, if ever needed, is a shared framework — i.e. the module split above). `SKIP_QUICKLOOK=1` builds without it, and the store copy must then drop the claim. **A PREVIEW IS NOT A READ, and the reader's own two best properties are wrong here.** (a) Laying the whole document out for an honest scrollbar (invariant 49) costs **3,022 ms** on a real 523 KB / 292,868-character Markdown file before anything can be seen — so the preview renders the HEAD of the source and says so. The bound is measured, same file, same run: **1,000 chars → 79 ms · 10,000 → 170 · 20,000 → 168 · 40,000 → 288 · 80,000 → 814**, i.e. 79 ms is the floor of building a window controller at all and 20,000 costs what 10,000 does — hence `previewSourceLimit`. A cut is taken back to a blank line and CLOSES an open code fence, or every remaining line renders as code. (b) An office document cannot be cut by its bytes, so it is cut by BLOCKS after reading, and the split says why that is worth doing: on a 20 MB HWPX the parse is 1,220 ms and the BUILD 1,998 ms — the typography, not the parser, is what a glance was paying for (3,973 → 1,727 ms end to end; the parse is irreducible, rhwp returns a whole document or nothing). (c) The page model is thrown away for the preview (`flattenPagesForPreview`) — the owner's instruction, *"좌우 페이지 여백 이런 게 안 보이고 오히려 콘텐츠만 보이면 돼"*, and correct: reproducing the author's paper spends a small panel on margins, and a table reflowing a little rougher was explicitly accepted. Both shortenings name themselves on screen (`shortenedNote`); a preview that silently stops early reads as a broken document. (d) The reading-line band is OFF in the preview (`ReaderTextView.showsReadingCursor`, set false by `QuickLookPreviewController`). It is the reader's "you are here", and a preview has no place to keep — on a real `.hwp` the caret sits at character 0, so the band landed across the document's TITLE and read as a highlight the file does not have. Same judgement `isDrawingToScreen` already makes for paper, made for the other medium that is not the reader; the flag defaults true so the reader is untouched, and the test asserts BOTH directions (mutation-checked). Which removed the only way to tell WHICH extension drew a preview, so (e) puts it back deliberately, and (f) uses the same mechanism for the other thing a preview must say about itself. **(e)** `PreviewBadgeView` lays translucent text over the panel — "⚡️FastDoc" top-right — installed as a SIBLING of the document's content view, never in the text storage, or ⌘F would answer it and a selection would copy it. It exists only on this path, so the reader, paper and `--pdf` are untouched by construction rather than by a flag. Two things it was WRONG about first: a filled capsule reads as a control and, at the panel's edge, drew clipped under the vertical scroller (hence text alone, and a 22pt right margin that clears it); and its test asserted the STRING, which is fake-green because `demo/code-blocks.md`'s own prose contains "FastDocReader" — it asserts the view hierarchy instead. **(f)** A shortened preview says so IN THE CONTENT — `shortenedNote` is `[ ... more in the full document ]`, appended as the last paragraph — and nowhere else. A pinned bottom-centre mark saying the same was built and REMOVED on sight: it does not move when the reader scrolls, so it reads as chrome rather than as the end of the text. The office budget is SHORTER than the text one (`previewOfficeCharacterBudget` 8,000 against 20,000), and it is in CHARACTERS on purpose even though the request was "five pages": measured through `--pdf`, a table-heavy `.docx` holds **233 characters per page** while Korean prose runs past a thousand, so a page-counting rule would cut one document at 1,200 characters and another at 9,000. Both marks are mutation-checked. **A rejected fix:** a narrow column-view panel showed a giant clipped heading, which looked exactly like the document staying at the placeholder 820pt width — measured, and it is NOT: resizing the controller's view to 300pt re-solves the container to 219pt with no help from us (`viewDidLayout` re-wrapping was built, measured to change nothing, and removed). What that panel actually showed was an H1 whose single word `Moby-Dick;` is wider than the column.

69. **A file type macOS was never told about cannot be bound to this app at all — and `DocumentTypes` alone tells it nothing.** `.conf`/`.cfg`/`.ini`/`.env` were in `plainTextExtensions` for a long time while `Info.plist` named no extension for them, so the panel opened them happily and the Finder could not: with no declaration anywhere, macOS types the file as a throwaway `dyn.…` and REFUSES to make any app its handler (`duti -s … vars all` → error -50, which surfaced the whole thing). `.txt`/`.csv`/`.log` escaped it only because OTHER apps register them as public types. The fix is a declaration we own — `UTExportedTypeDeclarations` (**exported**, not imported: `ai.ww-w.fast-md-reader.config-text` is in our namespace) — plus that identifier in a `CFBundleDocumentTypes` entry, because declaring a type only says it EXISTS while the document-type entry says we open it. Verified end to end, not by reading the plist: `duti -x vars` now answers this app, the previously failing `duti -s` succeeds, and a `.vars` file double-clicked in the Finder opens in a window of this build (`.ini` still goes to whichever editor the user already made default — correct, we are `Alternate`). `DocumentTypeDeclarationTests` reads the REAL `Info.plist` and fails in BOTH directions (an opened extension that is undeclared; a declared extension the app would refuse) — the CONVENTION §3 mirror made mechanical, mutation-checked both ways. **Three more things were measured while making the app the default handler for everything it opens, and all three contradicted what the plist assumed.** (a) The type a file really gets is not the one the name suggests: `.log` is `com.apple.log` (NOT `public.log`), `.cfg` is `public.toml`, `.ini` is `com.microsoft.ini` — read them with `UTType(filenameExtension:)` and claim THOSE, because a type someone else owns will not take our tags. (b) An office format has to be claimed as the **viewer** role (`duti -s … <ext> viewer`): the office entries are `Viewer` by invariant 22, so asking for the editor role is refused, silently. (c) `.mdown`/`.mkd`/`.mdtext` resolve to a throwaway `dyn.…` and were therefore **dropped from the app** rather than kept alive with an exported type of our own — the owner's call. And `.hwp` cannot be taken through the OLD API — `LSSetDefaultRoleHandlerForContentType` returns `noErr` and writes NOTHING to `com.apple.launchservices.secure.plist` when another installed app owns the type (Hancom here). Get Info ▸ Change All has always worked and was verified from the other side (after the owner did it, `duti -x hwp`/`hwpx` both answer `/Applications/FastDocReader.app`).

  **CORRECTED 2026-08-07 — the macOS 14 API is not that API, and it DOES take `.hwp`.** `NSWorkspace.setDefaultApplication(at:toOpen:)` does not fail silently on a type someone else owns: macOS puts up its OWN confirmation ("'.hwp' 확장자를 가진 모든 문서를 'FastDoc (Dev)'(으)로 열겠습니까?") naming both apps, and on approval the association changes. Seen on screen while claiming from the first-run guide. Scope of the measurement, stated exactly: the incumbent was the SHIPPED FastDoc rather than Hancom, so what is proven is "another installed app owns it", not "Hancom specifically" — but the refusal is the old API's, and repeating "HWP cannot be taken" is now wrong. **The consequence is the part to design around: that confirmation is per TYPE, and they STACK.** A single "Word and OpenDocument" tick spans five types, so a reader who ticks all four families can face a dozen system dialogs in a row. They are macOS's, not ours — nothing here can batch or suppress them, so the only lever is how many types a family spans.

70. **A sandboxed headless run reaches a file ONLY through a restored folder grant, and the restore has to happen before any entry point looks at a path.** The App Store build attaches no access to a path handed in on the command line, so `--extract`/`--pdf` were dead there — the fix is one line, `FolderAccess.restoreGrants()` at the top of `main.swift` (it used to sit in `applicationDidFinishLaunching`, which the headless flags never reach). Measured on a SANDBOX=1 build: with `~/Downloads` granted through the app's own panel, `--extract` reads a file **never opened before** and `--pdf` writes into that folder (21 pages); outside the granted folder both still refuse, now with a message that says what would fix it (`FolderAccess.annotatingHeadlessDenial`, the one place both CLIs compose it). Mutation-checked. So the honest claim for the store is "works in folders you have granted", NOT "does not work". **That claim was still FALSE for `--pdf` in 1.2, because a grant this app holds does not reach the process that writes the PDF.** `NSPrintInfo.AttributeKey.jobSavingURL` is honoured by the PRINT SUBSYSTEM, not by us, so the security-scoped access `restoreGrants()` opens never applies to it: measured on the shipped 1.2 build, printing to `~/Documents/_fdtest1.pdf` — **directly inside an already-granted folder** — failed with `PMSessionBeginCGDocumentNoDialog() returned -61`, as did a deeper path under the same grant, while the identical print into the container (`…/Data/tmp/`) produced a correct 9-page, 76,149-byte PDF. The entitlements were all present (`app-sandbox`, `print`, `files.user-selected.read-write`, `bookmarks.app-scope`) and the INPUT read fine from that same tree, so the app reached `op.run()` with the document fully laid out and died only on the write. The fix is one indirection, in `HeadlessPDF.writePDF`: print to `NSTemporaryDirectory()` (inside the container when sandboxed, ordinary temp otherwise — ONE path, not a branch only one build exercises), then `FileManager.moveItem` it to the destination, which the APP does and the grant therefore covers. **The grant hint moved with it** — it annotates only the move's failure, never the print's, because a print failure can no longer be about the user's folders. Ungranted destination, measured after the fix: exit 1 in **2.0 s** naming the real refusal, against a hang that had to be `pkill`ed. **AppKit answers a failed print with a modal alert, and a headless process never dismisses it** (`No NSAlertAction found for modalResponse: 0`, then nothing — no event loop, `.prohibited` activation), which is why the staging directory's writability is checked BEFORE `op.run()`: the remaining print-failure paths are the print stack's own, not ours to trigger. `swift test` is structurally blind to all of this (it runs unsandboxed, so `FolderAccess.isNeeded` is false) — what it DOES pin is that an unwritable destination still yields a real PDF and fails only at `couldNotPlace`, which the pre-fix shape cannot reach; mutation-checked, and the mutation **hangs instead of going red**. `Scripts/smoke-headless-pdf.sh` is the half a test cannot cover: run it against `SANDBOX=1 ./Scripts/make-app.sh release` before shipping. Two things this cost: the grant panel's own menu item is named ONCE (`FolderAccess.grantMenuTitle`) so the headless hint cannot name a menu item that no longer exists, and **verifying any of this through System Events is a trap while two identically-named apps are running** — `first process whose unix id is N` returned the OTHER process (the installed release, unsandboxed), so three separate menu readings disagreed and all were wrong; the app dumping its own `NSApp.mainMenu` to a log settled it in one run.

71. **"Where am I" is ONE toggle whose unit belongs to the document, drawn where it cannot touch the document, and reachable by typing the number back.** `MarginNumberStore.unit(paged:drawingPages:)` decides it once — pages for a paged document that is DRAWING its pages, lines for everything else, including a paged document whose outline is off (the owner's *"페이지로 나눠보기 옵션이 없을 때에는 라인 번호로 통일"*) — and the menu title, the painters and every test read that one answer instead of re-deriving it. Line numbers are `MDAttr.blockId + 1`, one per block, aligned to the glyphs' CAP TOP (a line fragment includes its leading, so aligning to `rect.minY` floats the number above the words it labels, visibly on a heading), and a line inside a table gets none — a cell is a grid position, and numbering the 10,021 table lines of a real report drowns the 4,524 that are its text. **Both painters are confined to what is already laid out**, because the way a decoration changes a document is not by writing to the storage but by ASKING a question that makes layout from inside a draw pass (`glyphRange(forBoundingRect:)`, `ensureLayout`, `usedRect`), and `MarginNumberTests` asserts every line sits identically with the numbers on and off.

    **(a) Page numbers are on the DESK, and they SHRINK before they disappear.** A number inside the sheet competes with the document's own header/footer and reads as content the file does not have, so `PageNumberDeskView` is a sibling laid over the scroll view — no text storage, no layout manager, structurally unable to move the document. But the desk is only what is left of the window after the paper, and at the reading zoom the paper routinely fills it, so a fixed 72pt number simply was not drawn ("좌우 여백이 아주 크지 않으면 안 보임"). `placement(number:deskWidth:)` walks a fixed ladder — 44 with a generous margin from the paper, then 44/32/24 against the gutter — and only when none fit does it fall back to just INSIDE the paper's own left margin, fainter. The 44pt ceiling is the owner's, set by looking at it: 72 and 56 read as too big. Stepped rather than scaled by a ratio: a numeral's width is not proportional to its point size, so a ratio has to be re-measured anyway, and a ladder keeps the size consistent from page to page.

    **(b) Typing a number IS the command.** Digits go to `DocumentWindowController.jumpBuffer` (four of them — a 490-page report exists in this project's own test set, so three would make its last pages unreachable), Return jumps, Escape or 1.8s of silence forgets, and `JumpIndicatorView` echoes it over the scroll view. Free to take because the view accepts no typing — the same reason the bare letter keys are — and Return/Escape are only consumed when something HAS been typed. ⌘L is the same jump for a reader who prefers to be asked. A LINE jump resolves through `MarginNumberNavigator.characterIndex(forLine:in:)`, by the rule the margin DRAWS by, so a number no line carries (every table block is missing from the sequence) lands on the next one that is drawn rather than beeping; a PAGE jump goes through `scrollSheetToTop`, never `scrollCharToTop`, because a page boundary belongs to the grid — a page's first character sits one leading band below the paper's edge, so jumping by character would cut the top margin, and the header living in it, off every page.

72. **A piece's inset is measured in a DIFFERENT frame from the one it is applied in, and that alone put a page break through a table's rows.** Reported on `사업계획서_13-15p_수정안.docx`: the desk gap between two sheets was drawn straight across the table's own lines. Measured — layout recorded `openedBands[0] = (top 887.43, height 23.17)` while lines at `870.71…890.88` and `890.88…911.05` were drawn inside that supposedly empty band, and the record was reproduced exactly by a from-scratch re-layout, so it was not stale. The cause is that **a cell's vertical alignment is applied AFTER the line fragment is placed**: the line at character 482 sits at `723.08` in the finished layout (its cell is centred in a 623.81pt row) and is proposed to `PageBandLayoutDelegate` at `423.94`. `TableMetrics.topInset` was measured from the finished layout (302.14) and subtracted from the PROPOSED rect, putting the piece's top 302pt above where it is drawn — so the rule judged a piece running to 911pt as ending at 745.6, declined to move it, and `pushWholeTable` then opened a boundary over content that was still there. The fix is one substitution: the delegate records every table line's top AFTER whatever that pass did to it (`proposedTableLineTops`), and `DocumentWindowController.laidOutTables` measures each row's `firstLineTop` from that record, so the inset is taken in the frame it will be used in.

    **(a) Two more things had to change for that document to paginate like Word, and both were measured.** (i) An entry already in `pushedTables` is RE-MEASURED each round even though it no longer overruns — it no longer overruns BECAUSE it was moved, and its metrics came from the layout that existed before anything moved. Only the KEY stays verbatim; invariant 61d's convergence argument is about not DROPPING a key, and refreshing a value converges as soon as the layout does (every number is rounded to a hundredth, and the whole record is compared each round). (ii) A piece that fits on NO page is named by character extent (`PagePagination.oversizedPieces`) and handed to the ordinary between-page rule, one line at a time — the ONE case where a table line is shifted where it stands. That is what Word does: `w:cantSplit` is what asks it not to split a row, and the reported file declares it **0 times** (and `w:vMerge` 0 times, so invariant 61a's tearing hazard cannot arise there).

    **(b) The numbers, one window (1199×893, outline ON, Split Tables OFF), before → after.** Lines drawn inside a band layout claimed was empty, and table lines left in a margin: `사업계획서_13-15p_수정안.docx` **2 → 0** and **9 → 0** (and 1 → 2 boundaries opened, i.e. the 3 pages Word shows); `2025_행정업무운영편람_최종.hwp` **151 → 11** and **566 → 15**; `1790387_prep_final_report.hwpx` 0 → 0 and **33 → 0**; `OpenAPI활용가이드_특일정보_v1.4.docx` 0 → 0 and **12 → 1**; `tago-tables.odt` 0 → 0 and 0 → 0. Turning JUST the over-tall-piece breaking off again, on the same build: 15 → 483, 0 → 33, 1 → 12 — so that half carries most of the margin repair, and it is what leaves the 11 remaining band overlaps on 편람 (0 without it). 483 rows in a margin against 11 lines under a band is not a close call, and invariant 64f's residue is what those 11 are.

    **(c) A "the gap is only real if nothing is drawn in it" suppressor was built and REMOVED.** Tracking the deepest line placed so far in the pass and refusing to record a band below it fixed the same document — and `PagedTableOverrunTests.testAMeasuredTableMovesWholeToTheNextPageTop` went red, correctly: the counter is not reset by a partial re-layout, so a stale high-water mark suppresses legitimate bands. With the frame fixed, every number above is identical without it, so it is not in the shipped code.

    **(d) What the tests can and cannot reach.** Mutation-checked, all four: breaking off → 2 failures, recording the proposal BEFORE the pass moves it → 16, freezing the metrics again → 1. The fourth — `laidOutTables` ignoring the record — bit NOTHING, because reaching it needs a real document whose cells are vertically centred, which this repo does not ship. `testAVerticallyCentredCellIsDrawnBelowWhereItsLineWasPlaced` pins the FACT the substitution stands on instead (a centred cell is drawn more than 10pt below where its line was placed) and is mutation-checked; the wiring itself is covered only by the measurements in (b).

    **(f) THE FINAL RULE, given by the owner on sight of the half-empty page: a table taller than the page is broken WHERE IT STANDS, and only a run of THREE LINES OR FEWER is moved on.** *"3줄 이하일 때만 다음 페이지로 넘겨야지 왜 통째로 넘겨"* / *"셀 중간이 잘리는 경우에는 라인이 없고, 셀로 구분되어 잘릴 때는 라인이 있는 것은 동일"*. Three parts, and the third is what made the first two work:
    (i) `PagePagination.oversizedPieces` takes every piece of a table whose OWN height exceeds the page body, not only the pieces that are themselves over-tall, and `tablesToPush` stops registering them (a piece asked to be carried is carried before anything can break it).
    (ii) `PageBandLayoutDelegate.pullToNextPage` + `DocumentWindowController.orphanRunStarts` move a run of ≤3 lines rather than break above it, measured from the completed layout because "how many of this piece's lines are on this page" cannot be asked while they are being placed.
    (iii) **A cell inside a broken piece is forced to TOP alignment** (`topAlignCellsOf`). Without it the reported document's centred left-hand label `유사 특허 / 분석 정확도` sat at 786.1…804.7 — inside the empty band running 784.5…910.6, i.e. floating on the desk between two sheets — because the middle of a row that spans a page break IS the gap. Word centres such a label within the part of the row on each page, which AppKit cannot be asked for; the row's top is the honest approximation.

    **Measured, same window as (b), after all three:** the reported docx fills `728 / 644` of a 728.5pt body (it was `360 / 620 / 384`), with **0** lines in a margin and **0** over a band; `2025_행정업무운영편람_최종.hwp` 20 in a margin and 7 over a band (against 14 / 10 when the same tables were carried — the trade the owner's rule asks for); `1790387_prep_final_report.hwpx` 0 / 0; `tago-tables.odt` 0 / 0. Two tests that pinned the OLD answer (each row of an over-tall table is CARRIED) were rewritten to the new one rather than deleted.

    **(e) Filling the page a carried row leaves half-empty was BUILT, MEASURED and REJECTED — for tables that FIT on a page.** The reported document ends up with page bodies carrying `360 / 620 / 384` of 728.5pt, because a 623.81pt row that will not fit on the rest of page 0 is carried whole to page 1 — Word instead splits that row and fills the page. Splitting every SINGLE-ROW piece in place (provably free of invariant 61a's tear: a vertical merge welds the rows it spans into one group, so a one-row group has no merged cell crossing it) does fill the page — and puts the rows back in the margin, because a line inside an `NSTextTableBlock` does not stay where this delegate puts it (invariants 39/42). Measured on one build, table lines left in a margin: `2025_행정업무운영편람_최종.hwp` **15 → 94**, `1790387_prep_final_report.hwpx` **0 → 7**, the reported docx **0 → 3**, with 507pt page bodies carrying 780–1015pt of lines. Over-tall pieces are the exception that works, and the reason is structural rather than lucky: they have nowhere to be carried to, so the choice there is between a broken row and a row in the margin, not between a broken row and a whole one. That is why (f) scopes breaking to tables that exceed a page and leaves every smaller table to the menu's own choice (invariant 64).

    **(g) The cut is drawn by `GridTextTableBlock`, and "the band crosses this frame" is NOT the test.** `NSTextTableBlock` paints one border box around the whole block, so a cell a page passes through gets a rule drawn across the desk. The subclass splits the frame at each opened band and omits the edges AT the cut (side rules and shading stay, so the two halves read as one cell continuing). The first version asked only whether a band fell inside the frame, and that erased every rule of a table that merely FOLLOWED a carried piece: measured by rendering the view and scanning it for long dark rows, `사업계획서_13-15p_수정안.docx` came back with no horizontal rule anywhere between view-y 448 and 1561 — a full-grid table drawn with no grid. **And the two sides speak different coordinate systems**: `frameRect` arrives in the VIEW's coordinates while `openedBands` is recorded from line fragment rects, which are the TEXT CONTAINER's — `NSTextView.textContainerOrigin`, 28pt here. Comparing them raw put every cut 27.6pt too high: scanning the rendered view for its vertical rules, they stopped at text-y 756.9 and resumed at 882.7 against a band of 784.5…910.6, leaving a strip of cell painted on the desk and a strip of blank paper above the text that follows. With the origin added, the rules stop at 784.7 and resume at 910.4 — the band exactly, which is also where the text is cut, because a band IS `[where the moved line was going to start, where it now starts]`.

    Two further shapes were built and measured wrong before that. **Deriving the cut per CELL** (each cell ending at its own last line) is what the owner's *"선과 셀의 배경이 텍스트 잘리는 부분과 y 위치가 맞아야지"* seems to ask for and is not: a label whose text stops 300pt above its row's bottom was drawn as a 6.4pt sliver (`segs=["442.5…448.9", "910.6…1198.8"]`). The band already aligns with the text, for every cell of the row at once. And **requiring the cell's own text on both sides of the band** — the first attempt at "is this really a cut" — left the tall label cell painting its shading and side rules straight across the desk and on into the next sheet, which is the defect the owner reported as *"페이지를 넘어간 바탕화면까지 그려지잖아"*. Nothing is ever painted inside a band; whether the cell's text continues on the far side decides nothing.

73. **A Korean document's page is defined per SECTION, and the first section is usually the cover — take the geometry of the section that holds the most of the document.** `document_json.rs`'s `page_geometry_pt` read `document.sections.first()`, so a 14-section file was typeset on its title page's margins. Measured on `2025_행정업무운영편람_최종.hwp` (rhwp's own render is the reference, and OUR pinned 0.7.19 CLI produces it: `rhwp export-pdf` → **394 pages**): section 0 declares body `396.9×507.4pt` with a top margin of `138.9`, while sections 2–10 — where 2,551 of the document's 2,618 paragraphs live — declare `395.7…396.9 × 555.6pt` with a top margin of `110.6`. rhwp's own render tree for a body page names exactly that (`Body {x 113.4, y 147.4, w 529.1, h 740.8}` px at 96dpi = `85.05 / 110.6 / 396.8 / 555.6` pt), so the body section's numbers ARE the renderer's numbers, and the cover's are not. Picking the section with the most paragraphs (ties → the earlier one, which `max_by_key` alone would NOT give: it returns the LAST maximum) took our `--pdf` of that file from **458 pages to 426**. The residual against rhwp's 394 is NOT geometry — the body area now matches to about a point — and belongs to how much text a line and a table consume, which is a separate measurement. **A viewer gets ONE reading column per document (invariant 57), which is why this is a choice of section rather than support for several**; two of the 14 sections are landscape (`754×555.6` paper), and honouring those needs the structural change invariant 57 forbids doing casually.

74. **HWP states a cell's borders through an id, and that id is the ONLY thing that draws a rule — a table's own fill is its background.** Every HWP cell carries a `borderFillId` into `DocInfo`'s border-fill table; the exporter emitted the id but never the table, so the id resolved to nothing and every HWP table was ruled with the reader's own theme grid. Measured on the same 편람: **821 border-fill definitions, 423 of them with all four edges `type 0` (no line)** — the layout boxes a Korean document uses to place things had their grid drawn back on by us. The fill table now ships as a document-level array (`borderFills`, indexed `borderFillId - 1`; HWP's reference is 1-based and `0` means "unnamed"), the same shape and for the same reason as `charShapes`, and each edge arrives as a type plus width in POINTS (HWP stores a 16-step mm enum; converting at the source keeps the host from carrying that table). A resolved edge is always a DECLARATION — `none` becomes `.suppressed`, never silence — because silence falls back through invariant 47's cascade to the very rule the document erased.

    **A table's own border-fill is its BACKGROUND, and that had to be measured rather than reasoned.** The first version of this work stamped the table's fill onto the perimeter cells as a box, on the strength of one document where the cells were all-off and a box was visibly there. Rendering the same page from rhwp's own SVG settled it the other way: on the page carrying that box there is **not one stroke element** — the box is drawn as NINE image tiles (the table's fill is a PICTURE fill), and on the 기안/협조/결재 table, whose table fill declares four solid edges, rhwp draws only the CELLS' dotted rules and no solid box at all. rhwp's renderer passes the table's fill to `render_cell_background` and to nothing else (`layout/table_cell_content.rs:529`). So `HwpReader` maps the table's id to `TableFormat.defaultShading` and never to a border; a picture fill has no shading colour and is honestly absent (that box does not render until image fills are supported). The stamping code and its two tests are gone — what stands in their place is `testTableOwnFillIsShadingNeverABorder`.

    **What HWP still loses, measured on the same file: the LINE STYLE.** Its fills use `dot` 59 times and `double` 13 times against 1,025 `solid`, and `BorderSide` carries only width and colour — so a dotted rule is drawn solid. This is not an HWP gap: `DocxReader` reads `w:val` only to tell `none`/`nil` from a rule (`DocxReader.swift:2791`) and `OdtReader` splits `fo:border`'s style token and drops it (`OdtReader.swift:1781`), so all three formats render every declared rule solid. Fixing it is one field on `BorderSide` plus dash support in `GridTextTableBlock`'s own painting, since `NSTextTableBlock` draws only solid edges.

75. **A Korean document's drawings arrive as PATHS, and only the ones placed AS CHARACTERS may be drawn inline.** rhwp's exporter used to answer every drawing object with `{"label":"shape","w":0,"h":0}` — no geometry, not even a size — so a page whose diagram is four labels joined by six arrows rendered as four labels in empty space (78 shapes in `2025_행정업무운영편람_최종.hwp`, 322 paths between them). What ships instead is rhwp's OWN reduction: its renderer flattens line/rect/ellipse/arc/polygon/curve/group to move/line/curve/close before drawing anything (`renderer/layout/shape_layout.rs`), and the export now carries exactly that, plus the stroke (colour, width in points, the same four line styles a border edge uses), a solid fill, and the two arrowhead flags from the border line's attribute bits (10–15 start, 16–21 end). One thing to draw instead of nine, and no second opinion about what an `arc_type` means.
    **Drawn at READ time, into a PDF, so the vocabulary learns nothing.** `HwpShapeRenderer` renders the paths into PDF bytes and `HwpReader` hands them to the media map under a `hwpshape:` id — from there a drawing IS an embedded picture to everything downstream (reservation, reconcile, sizing, `--extract`), so no new `OfficeBlock` case, no builder branch, no serializer arm. PDF rather than a bitmap because the reader zooms; an arrowhead rasterised at 16pt is a smear at 32. Arrowheads are this reader's geometry, not the document's — HWP states only WHICH head a line has — and are scaled to the rule's own width so a hairline connector does not carry a barb the size of a glyph.
    **Three things the export got wrong the first time, all measured on the same file.** (i) A shape's points are stored in the object's ORIGINAL space and its `ShapeComponentAttr` carries six render coefficients that place them — the renderer applies that matrix (`layout/shape_layout.rs`'s `transform_pt`) and the export did not, so a real pair of arrows came out as `M 0,0 L 100,100`: 1pt specks, the matrix having carried all of their extent. (ii) A drawing that CONTAINS pictures is a screenshot in a frame, and exporting the frame while dropping the picture left three consecutive pages of a manual as an empty red rectangle — the picture is the content, so a shape with pictures now yields the PICTURES and no frame box. **And that picture usually states NO SIZE of its own** — the size is on the shape component holding it (`current_width`/`current_height`) — so emitting the zero made the host reserve nothing and the figure was simply absent from the page; two screenshots of that manual vanished that way, and the pages they belonged to ended early enough that their numbering looked wrong to a reader comparing with the original. (iii) A LINE record still carries a fill colour — white, in these documents — so filling every path painted white bars across the text and a table's own column label vanished under one; only a path that CLOSES is filled.

    **The gate is `treat_as_char`, and it is not a detail.** HWP anchors most objects by coordinates, and this reader has no floating layer (invariant 31 — built, measured, deliberately not shipped). Drawing an anchored object inline does not put it where the document put it; it INSERTS it. Measured: several of that manual's shapes are the size of the whole page (555×752pt), and inlining all of them took the document from 423 pages to **452**. An anchored drawing is therefore skipped — the same nothing the reader showed before it could draw at all — while an as-character one is drawn where it belongs. A TEXT BOX's words are never at risk either way: rhwp emits them as SIBLING blocks, so they arrive as ordinary paragraphs whatever happens to the frame. **A floating layer for the paragraph-anchored ones was BUILT and REMOVED**: 17 of that manual's 33 anchored objects are `para`-relative, which this layout CAN locate, so each was painted over the text from a zero-width carrier — and the offsets alone turned out not to be the placement. `vert_align`/`horz_align` decide what an offset is measured FROM, and without them a 431pt decorative rule landed on the table's own `< 기 안 >` column label and erased it. The arrows stay undrawn until that geometry is done properly; these numbers are here so the next attempt starts from them rather than from scratch.

76. **A fill can be a PICTURE, and in Korean documents it usually is.** Measured on the same manual: of 821 border-fill definitions, **352 are picture fills** — used by 610 cells and 55 tables — against 53 solid colours and no gradients at all. The rounded annotation frames and tinted panels a Korean document is built out of are images, not colours, so a reader that knows only `bg` renders them as blank paper (which is exactly what this one did while claiming to honour the document's shading). The export now carries `bgImage` (a `binDataId`) and `bgGradient` (stops + angle) beside `bg`, and `Cell.backgroundImage`/`TableFormat.backgroundImage` carry the decoded picture.
    **Where each is painted matters.** A CELL's picture is drawn by `GridTextTableBlock`, per page segment, so a cell a page break crosses paints its image on each sheet and nothing on the desk between them. A TABLE's picture is drawn ONCE by `GridTextTable` across the whole grid — repeating it per cell turns one rounded frame into a wall of frames. The bytes need the parse handle, so `mapJSON` takes a picture PROVIDER rather than reaching for the FFI: with no provider (every unit test) the fill is simply unpainted, never guessed at. A gradient degrades to its first stop, because a panel washed in one of its own colours is closer to the document than blank paper.

77. **A running head belongs to ITS OWN section, and applying one document-wide puts a page number on 400 pages that never had it.** Reported as "페이지 번호가 위/아래로 번갈아 찍힌다": measured on `2025_행정업무운영편람_최종.hwp`, every EVEN page carried a number at the top (y≈700 of 754) and every ODD page one at the bottom (y≈50), perfectly alternating over 400 pages. The cause is not the bands and not parity handling. Exactly **ONE of that document's 14 sections declares any running head at all** — section 11, a five-paragraph LANDSCAPE insert, with one header (`applyTo=even`) and two footers — and the exporter collected them into two document-level arrays, which the reader then painted on every page of the flattened flow. rhwp itself draws **neither**: its own render tree for a body page has an empty `Header` and an empty `Footer`, and the number a reader sees there comes from the **바탕쪽 (master page)** — a `Rect > TextBox` at (595, 906) px, bottom-right, on every page.
    The fix is to say WHICH section each entry came from (`section` on every header/footer entry) and which section the document is typeset on (`bodySection` — the same most-paragraphs choice invariant 73 makes for the page itself), and to keep only the entries whose section matches. One rule, one source: the page and its running heads now come from the same section rather than from different ones. A parser predating both fields leaves them absent and every entry is kept, unchanged. Measured after: the 386 top/bottom numbers become **zero**, which is the honest state — this document's numbers live on a master page, and **바탕쪽 support is what would restore them** (it also carries the side tabs and header rules the same document draws). Mutation-checked: dropping the filter reddens the test by name.

78. **A Korean document's page number is not in a header — it is on the 바탕쪽, and drawing that means drawing objects on the SHEET rather than laying a flow into a band.** Invariant 77 ended with the honest state that this manual's 434 pages carried no number at all, because the only running head its file declares belongs to a five-paragraph landscape insert. rhwp's own render puts the number on every page from the master page, and so does this reader now. What the mechanism is, measured on `2025_행정업무운영편람_최종.hwp`: **13 master pages, of which the body section's `both` template holds four objects** — the running title (a text box at (55.16, 58.12) pt, 338.46 × 16.62), the full-page artwork (a picture at (0, 0), 555.58 × 760.80), the tab down the outer edge (a drawing at (500.62, 218.55), 64.83 × 113.68) and the number box (a text box at (446, 679.86), 37 × 22, whose span carries the same `pageNumberField` a running header's does). Every one is PAPER-relative, which is what makes them drawable where an anchored object is not (invariant 75): there is no anchor to resolve and no flow to consult, so each object is placed on the sheet rectangles `PagePagination` already computes for the screen and the printer alike (invariant 59) and nothing is reserved, measured or laid out. `MasterPagePainter` is therefore NOT a second `PageBandPainter`; it shares only the field substitution. Measured after: **all 434 pages carry their own number**, at x 455–463 and y 60.8 of 754 — bottom right, constant, matching rhwp's own render.
    Three things this cost, each of which looked like a rendering bug and was not. **(a) Stored order is not draw order.** The artwork is stored FIRST and the title SECOND, so drawing them as stored painted a full-page picture over the title on every page. rhwp sorts paper-relative nodes by `(plane, z_order, stable_index)` where plane is the text-wrap band; the export now carries `plane`/`z` and the reader sorts by the same key rather than inventing one. **(b) A shape's border comes from its LINE TYPE, not its width.** HWP states the two separately and type 0 is "선 없음": the title box and the number box each declare type 0 with a width of 33, and an exporter asking only `width > 0` boxed both on all 434 pages while rhwp draws neither. The same rule's other half is the one that would have been missed by simply inverting the test — a declared line with width 0 still draws, at Hancom's own 0.12 mm minimum, and a dashed one is never thinner than a pixel. **(c) A degenerate box is not a size to invent.** One master text box (the cover section's rotated tab label) states a width of ZERO; it is dropped rather than given a width this reader chose, which is invariant 57's rule restated. The body section's own four objects all state real boxes, so nothing is lost by it: the file's ONE zero-width box belongs to another section's template and carries no spans at all. The vertical tab LABEL rhwp draws down the outer edge is the one piece of this page still missing, and it is not that box — the body section's tab object is a drawing with two paths and no text, so wherever those glyphs come from is not yet exported.
    **A master page belongs to its SECTION, and a reader that flattens every section into one column must still pick per PAGE.** Keeping only the body section's template — the rule invariant 77 needed for a running head — was reported on sight: that section's chapter title printed on the cover, on the table of contents and on 400 pages of other chapters. The flat export says nothing about where a section begins, so it now carries `sectionStarts` (the block index each section starts at); `OfficeTextBuilder` marks those blocks with `MDAttr.sectionIndex`, and a page's section is the LAST marker at or before the character the typesetter put at that page's top. Measured on the 편람 (14 sections, 13 templates between them): the title stops being constant and follows the section — 4 cover pages with none, 16 pages of `2025 행정업무운영 편람`, 164 of `제2장. 공문서 관리 등 행정업무의 처리`, and so on. Two of those sections declare no template at all and one declares a pair with no objects, so their pages draw nothing rather than borrowing a neighbour's — the appendix's own page numbers come from a FOOTER instead, which is still filtered to the body section (invariant 77) and is the next thing this needs.
    **The running head is the same rule one level down, and its own placement had to be fixed to survive it.** Once a page knows its section, the entries no longer need filtering to one section (invariant 77's answer while nothing could place a page): every section's are kept and chosen per page. Turning that on exposed two placement faults that had been invisible while only one section's entries existed, both measured on the same manual's appendix. (1) With NO declared footer distance the footer divided the GAP layout opened — and a page whose last line ends early opens a gap that begins in the middle of the sheet, so the page number printed at y≈451 of 754, across the body. (2) The header's mirror fallback put page 372's number at the BOTTOM of page 371's paper. Both now anchor to the SHEET EDGE whenever it is known (a missing distance means "as low as the paper allows" / "just below the paper's top"), which is the same paper-grid discipline the master page itself uses. After: each appendix page carries exactly one number at y≈18.6, alternating bottom on odd and top on even pages — which is what the document declares and what rhwp's own render does. The band's own height is now the TALLEST entry rather than the first, since any section's may be the one painted.
    Scope, deliberately: master pages ride the page-outline master switch (`PageViewOptions.underOutlineRule`) like every other piece of page furniture — with the outline off there is no sheet for them to be on — and they are NOT gated on drawing to screen, because a printout missing the page number is missing the document. Mutation-checked, both ways: removing the sort reddens two tests by name and removing the degenerate-box guard reddens one.

79. **Word states a page per SECTION too, and the body's TRAILING `w:sectPr` is the LAST section's.** Invariant 73 measured what taking the wrong section's page costs in HWP — a 394-page manual rendered as 458 — and fixed it there by choosing the section with the most paragraphs. The docx reader had the mirror image of the same bug and nobody had looked: `body.children.last(where: { $0.name == "w:sectPr" })` fed the page geometry, the line grid AND the running-head references, so a report whose final section is a one-page landscape appendix or a colophon typeset the ENTIRE document on that page. `DocxReader.typesetSectionProperties` now applies HWP's rule to Word: a section ends at the paragraph whose `w:pPr/w:sectPr` closes it (the body's own trailing one closes the last), the section holding the most paragraphs wins, and a TIE goes to the EARLIER one — `max(by:)` returns the last maximum, which is the same trap invariant 73 names for `max_by_key`. A table counts as content, so a section made of one long table does not read as empty. A single-section document — the overwhelming majority — has exactly one candidate and is byte-identical to before.

    **ODF states it a third way, and the same rule reaches it.** There is no section element at all: a page style changes when a paragraph applies a style carrying `style:master-page-name`, and every following paragraph stays on that master page until another switch. So the count is per MASTER PAGE — the first declared one is the default, an EMPTY `style:master-page-name` is a page break that keeps the current style (not a switch), and the switch is resolved through `style:parent-style-name` because a writer puts it on the named style while the paragraph names the automatic one (`P1`). The chosen master page then supplies BOTH the paper and the running header, so those two can never come from different page styles. Counting only the paragraphs that NAME a style is the trap: an ordinary paragraph declares none, so that count sees only the switch points and one appendix paragraph outvotes a whole body.
