Do not "promote" it into the suite — inside a full run it cannot fail, and a check that cannot fail is worse than no check because it reads as coverage.

    **`s5c1.md:86` had already named this failure mode** ("the measurer is not installed when the query runs") and the wiring shipped anyway. A plan recognising a hazard is not the same as the hazard being prevented; the prevention has to be a call in `Sources/` or a probe someone runs.

105. **THE MEASUREMENT PORT FINISHES A PARAGRAPH THROUGH THE HOST'S OWN TERMINATOR PASS, NEVER THROUGH A RESTATEMENT OF IT — and a corpus of single-font paragraphs will hide a restatement's error indefinitely.** `RustEngineMeasure.measure` rebuilds the string the engine flattened and must build the one the host would have built, terminator included (invariant 51: an unattributed `"\n"` falls to a default face and measured 14.0pt where the host's own build reported 13.0). The port originally attributed it with the LAST text run's font. The host's rule is the opposite end: `OfficeTextBuilder.unifyTerminator` copies `result.attributes(at: paragraph.location)` — the paragraph's START.

    **The two agree for almost every paragraph, because almost every paragraph is one font throughout** — which is why a real paged header measured correctly under the wrong rule for two sprints. It surfaced on an HWP FOOTNOTE, whose first run is the citation number at the document's own default size and whose last run is the note's smaller body text. TextKit measures its invisible trailing line fragment against whatever font that terminator carries, so the small last-run font made every note in `Vendor/rhwp-src/samples/footnote-01.hwp` measure **29.7pt against the host's 36.0** — 6.3pt short, nine notes out of nine, with a green suite.

    **A short band is the corrupt half (invariant 98).** Under-reserving puts a note over the last line of body text; over-reserving only wastes space. So a measurement disagreement in this direction is a defect even when it is small.

    **What finds it: a parity test on a real document whose paragraph is NOT one font.** `FootnoteHeightsDocumentPathTests.testTheRealEngineAndTheRealHostAgreeOnEveryNoteOfARealDocument` is that test; reverting the rule to the last run's font fails it on all nine notes. Synthetic fixtures and single-font headers cannot see it — the fixture has to mix sizes inside one paragraph.

    **The rule is not restated on this side — the host's own function is called.** `RustEngineMeasure` appends the terminator BARE and then hands the finished string to `OfficeTextBuilder.unifyParagraphTerminators`, the same pass the builder runs on its own output. A port that re-implements "the first run's font" is right for the paragraphs it was measured on and silently narrower everywhere else: `unifyTerminator` copies every INHERITABLE attribute, and it refuses the copy outright when the paragraph's first character carries one that is not (an attachment, a highlight, a link). A running header that opens with a logo is exactly that paragraph — the host leaves its terminator bare and a "first font" port would attribute it, and no amount of correct measuring on other documents would reveal the difference. Calling the one function removes the question instead of answering it. Mutation-checked: applying the pass to a COPY of the string instead of the string itself puts every note of the real document back to 32.0pt against the host's 36.0.

106. **A CROSS-CHECK WHOSE EXPECTED SIDE WALKS ONLY ONE LAYER CANNOT SEE THE OTHER — and it stays green for exactly as long as the first layer keeps refusing first.** Found in S6-4, measured rather than reasoned. `office_fallback_ledger_ffi.rs` compares the refusal ledger (written by the real FFI symbol) against the same refusals computed directly, per kind — deliberately not a non-emptiness assertion, because "forty-nine swallowed out of fifty" would pass that. But the FFI symbol walks TWO layers, adapter then export (`fastdoc-ffi/src/lib.rs:261`: `from_office` → `office_project::project`), and the expected side computed only the adapter's. A refusal swallowed in the export layer could never appear on the expected side, so the test had a hole the whole time.

    It surfaced only because a fixture MOVED between layers. `issue2083_hide_fill_page.hwpx` declares three sections; before S6-4 the adapter refused it first (`CellBackgroundImagePresent`) and `project` was never reached, so both sides counted one adapter refusal and agreed. S6-4 made the adapter accept it, the FFI went on to `project`, and it was refused there as `Field("sections")` — visible on the ledger side, absent from the expected side. Measured per fixture: `so-sueop.hwp` adapter-refuses `MissingResource("hwpshape:1")`; `tac-img-02.hwp` clears both; `issue2083` clears the adapter and is refused by the export.

    The fix computes the expected side through BOTH layers in the same order the symbol walks them. Proven to bite by deleting the export layer's `projection_ledger::record` — the old test could not fail on that mutation, the new one does. **The rule: when a check mirrors a pipeline, the mirror must have as many stages as the pipeline. A stage the mirror omits is untested precisely when an earlier stage stops shadowing it — which is what every successful refusal-reduction sprint causes.**

107. **A PRESENCE TEST PROVES THE VALUE ARRIVED, NEVER THAT NOTHING WAS DESTROYED GETTING THERE — and a ledger that calls a field "derived" is making the stronger claim without proving it.** Found in S6-7, traced rather than reasoned. `OfficeReadResult.default_body_font_size` is recorded as `Derived` (`office_result_accounting.rs:243`), defended by a test whose own doc comment states the standard: *"That is only true if a run which states no size of its own actually carries the document's default — otherwise the fact is simply dropped and the tree is not self-contained."* The test passes. The tree is still lossy.

    `convert_text_run` stamps a size on EVERY run (`office_adapter.rs:1465`, `span.font_size.or(Some(default_body_font_size))`), so inside the canonical tree a run that DECLARED 11pt and a run that INHERITED 11pt are the same bytes. The document's default is then thrown away — `wire.rs` has no field for it anywhere. Both facts the document stated are gone: what each run declared, and what the document's default was.

    The schema-v4 projection then tries to recover the lost bit by GUESSING: `mode_or` (`office_project.rs:179`) takes the most common run size as "the default" and `nullify_spans` (`:316`) nulls every span matching it. On a long document the guess is usually right. On a short one it is always wrong — measured on two real fixtures with exactly one run each, where that run's own explicit 11pt is trivially the mode and gets nulled, after which the reader substitutes the document default and renders docx at 20pt and odt at 26pt where both should be 22pt. The two miss in OPPOSITE directions for one reason, not two: 10×2 and 13×2 are those documents' real defaults.

    This outlives the transitional export. The canonical tree is the destination of the whole migration, and as designed it cannot express the distinction invariant 36 is built on — every absolute value sized to the DOCUMENT's own default, so the reader's zoom multiplies on top of it. **The rule: when a ledger says "derived", the test owed is not "the value is reachable" but "the source fact can still be recovered from what we kept". A default stamped onto every consumer is not carried — it is smeared, and smearing is indistinguishable from the consumers having declared it.**

108. **THE LAST BRANCH OF A FLAG CHAIN IS A CLAIM, NOT A DEFAULT — and an empty collection written where the truth is "we were not told" is the same mistake wearing a different shape.** Four instances found in one day (S6-4/S6-7), each of which had already cost a real defect:

    - `if same_width { Equal } else if proportional { Proportional } else { Absolute }` (`office_accounting.rs:562`) — the last arm asserts the document declared absolute per-column widths, when all it actually knows is that neither flag was set. Measured: `issue2019_floating_form_74312.hwpx` declares `colCount="2" sameSz="0"` and NOT ONE `<hp:colLine>` anywhere in the file, so it lands on `Absolute`, and `validate.rs` then demands a widths array the document never wrote. The shipped reader draws the same document as two equal columns (`ColumnGeometry.swift:69` — no widths listed, fall through to the equal split).
    - `bookmarks: vec![]` in `convert_run` (`office_project.rs:634`) — writes "this run has no bookmarks" for every run, whether or not it has one. Every internal link in the flag-on build goes nowhere.
    - `"comments": []` in `project` (`office_project.rs:222`) — writes "this document has no comments" unconditionally. The module's own doc comment claims a document with comments is refused; that claim is false for a comment with no body range, which is exactly the case that reached the hardcode.
    - the ledger's `Derived` for `default_body_font_size` — see invariant 107.

    A missing declaration and a declaration of nothing are DIFFERENT FACTS, and a slot that cannot hold the difference will be filled with whichever one the writer happened to have in mind. The tree then reads as authoritative about something nobody measured, and — this is the part that hides it — **it reads as authoritative in exactly the direction that makes the common case work**, so it stays green until an uncommon document arrives.

    **The rule: when a value's truth is "the source did not say", the tree must say THAT, positively — a named state, not an empty vector, not the last arm of an if-chain, not an `Option` that also means something else. And the same rule that makes the tree honest makes it match the product: the reader already draws all four of these cases without complaint, so every one of them was the canonical tree being stricter than the app it is replacing.**

109. **A TEST THAT REQUIRES A REAL DOCUMENT TO KEEP FAILING WILL EVENTUALLY BE SATISFIED ONLY BY PRESERVING A DEFECT.** Found in S6-5a, and it had already changed someone's behaviour before it was named. `office_projection_refusal_census.rs:330` asserts *"expected at least one fixture to be refused by from_office"* — a vacuity guard, written for the right reason (a census that can only ever report one outcome is measuring nothing, invariant 5's family). But it is anchored to the REAL corpus, so it passes only while some real document is still refused, and every sprint on this roadmap exists to make that number zero.

    The cost is not hypothetical. When S6-5a made the census's only refused fixture render (a declared picture with no bytes now reserves an empty box, matching the shipped reader), the response was to go looking for another still-broken document and add it — `59043_regulatory_analysis.hwp`, chosen because it was "untouched by S6-5a, so it keeps the branch honest". That document is the very next thing scheduled to be fixed (`Canonicalization("cell padding is invalid")`), so the same hunt would have run again. Two tests were left depending on the product staying defective, and the same reasoning appears in `office_fallback_ledger_ffi.rs`, whose refusing fixture had already been swapped once for the same reason.

    **Separate the two jobs the test was doing.** Measuring the real corpus is a report: tabulate by kind and print, with no floor under the failure count. Proving the machinery can tell the two outcomes apart is a vacuity guard, and it belongs on a SYNTHETIC input that refuses BY CONSTRUCTION — for this engine, a caller that deliberately withholds a resource key, which is the programming-error case `MissingResource` is meant to keep strict and which can never be "fixed away" because it is not a document's fact. A guard built that way is still honest on the day every real document is accepted.

    Where a synthetic refusal genuinely cannot be built — the FFI symbol takes real bytes, so its fallback test needs a real document that refuses — the test must say what its own failure MEANS: not "find another broken document" but "the fallback may now be unreachable; check whether that path is dead and delete it". A test whose green light depends on a bug must name that dependency, or the next person will read a red light as a request for a new bug.

110. **A DEPENDENCY'S CARGO CHECKOUT IS SHARED, MACHINE-GLOBAL, AND OUTSIDE EVERY `git status` — INSTRUMENTATION LEFT THERE IS INVISIBLE AND SURVIVES EVERYTHING.** Found in S6-6b, after two separate agents each concluded the other must have polluted the tree and both were innocent. A 400-document census kept printing `S6_5_PROBE cell(2,0) apply_inner_margin=… cell.padding=…` while a search of the entire worktree — source, `rust/target`, `Vendor`, binaries included — found the string nowhere. Both the lead and a probe agent ran `git status`, `git diff`, and `git grep HEAD`, all clean. The measurement was therefore declared untrustworthy and re-run on a "clean" tree; **it printed the same lines again.** That second run is the only reason it was found, and it was run only because the leak-check had been made an explicit step rather than an assumption.

    The edit was in `~/.cargo/git/checkouts/rhwp-56be70b771a6f870/001fd0b/src/document_core/queries/document_json.rs` — the cargo checkout of the pinned `rhwp` revision (`crates/fastdoc-engine/Cargo.toml`'s `git = …, rev = 001fd0b…`). That directory belongs to no repository the project can see: it is not in the worktree, not in the main checkout, not in `Vendor/rhwp-src`, and it is **shared by every project on this machine that pins the same revision**. Six added lines, an `eprintln!` under a threshold — which is what made the leak survivable rather than catastrophic, and is not something the next one is guaranteed to be.

    Three consequences, none of which are obvious from inside the repo. It is invisible to every repo-level hygiene check the project has, including invariant 30's. It outlives worktree cleanup, branch switches and fresh clones, because the checkout is not part of any of them. And it would be COMPILED INTO A SHIPPED ARTIFACT: the same revision builds `Vendor/RhwpNative.xcframework`, so a rebuild would carry the edit into the app.

    **Two rules follow.** First, a probe that must instrument a DEPENDENCY restores it in the same breath, and the restore is `git -C <checkout> checkout -- <file>` in that checkout, which is itself a git worktree of cargo's cached bare repo — that is what makes the restore verifiable rather than a hand-edit. Second, and more useful: **when output appears that the source cannot explain, do not conclude the source is clean — conclude the search was too narrow.** The tree was searched exhaustively and honestly, and the honest search still could not see the file, because the file was outside every boundary the search was built from. The question that found it was not "where in this repo is that string" but "what code is in this binary that is not in this repo".

    The corollary for measurement: a leak whose only effect is a print does not change a number, so the two censuses taken during the polluted window (`266/400` and `345/400`) were valid all along — but that could only be asserted AFTER reading the diff. Provenance is not established by a plausible argument about what the pollution probably was; it is established by finding it.

111. **A `-p <crate>` GATE IS NOT THE WORKSPACE GATE, AND THE CRATE IT OMITS IS WHERE THE BOUNDARY TESTS LIVE.** Found in S9-1, five sprints after the gate started lying. `CLAUDE.md` says `cd rust && cargo test`; the sprint's runner ran `cargo test -p fastdoc-engine` and reported its 278 passes as "cargo green". The workspace is 302, and the 24 it omits are `fastdoc-ffi`'s — the tests that drive the REAL exported symbols, which is exactly where a host meets this engine. `office_fallback_ledger_ffi` had been failing since `7ef0e0c` and no gate ever ran it.

    **What it was failing on is the second half of the finding.** The test cross-checks the refusal ledger against refusals computed directly, and it opens with a vacuity guard: *"this fixture set must contain at least one refusal for the cross-check to be meaningful."* Its only refusing fixture was `issue2083_hide_fill_page.hwpx`, refused as `Field("sections")` because it declares three sections. S6 gave a multi-section document's declarations a home, the refusal went away, and the guard fired — **correctly**. The guard did its job; nothing ran it.

    **The lesson was already written one function above it.** `a_refused_fixture_still_comes_back_...` carries a paragraph explaining that it deliberately uses a SYNTHETIC docx (`docx_zip_bytes_with_unresolvable_image`) rather than a real document, citing invariant 109: a test that needs a real document to STAY broken breaks every time that document gets fixed, and fixing it is this roadmap's whole job. The neighbouring test leaned on a real document's defect anyway. **A rule recorded in a file is not a rule applied in that file** — the fix here was to put the by-construction refusal first in the same fixture set, keeping the real HWP documents as the accepted side, because a cross-check that only ever saw refusals would be just as blind.

    **The rule: name the gate by what it must cover, never by a crate you happen to be working in.** A `-p` flag is a development convenience; it becomes a lie the moment its output is reported as the gate's verdict. Same shape as the `--skip OfficeDocumentTests` excuse (CLAUDE.md's own gate note) — a scope narrowed for a good reason on Monday is an unread scope by Friday.

112. **A PAYLOAD'S SIZE IS NOT ITS VOCABULARY'S SIZE — attribute the bytes before letting one number justify a rewrite.** Found in S9-3, where the number had already been carried through a whole sprint as settled. The roadmap's only argument against schema-v4 was one measurement: `2025_행정업무운영편람_최종.hwp` exports a **77,946,260-byte** JSON, written down as "a number S9 must not leave alone" when it retires `OfficeBlock`. The reading behind it was that the vocabulary is bloated and the canonical tree would be leaner. `office_payload_size_census.rs` measured the two exports side by side through the real FFI symbols and none of that survived:

    **69% of that document's export is picture payload** — 53,937,512 bytes of base64 that both vocabularies carry identically, because they carry the same pictures. The vocabulary's own share is 24,008,748. A vocabulary change cannot touch the other two thirds.

    **The tree is not smaller. Across `testdocs` its description is 1.51× schema-v4's** (56,086,702 vs 37,047,107 over the 16 documents both exports answered), and on `giant-table.odt` it is 4.65× (14,481,099 vs 3,114,648). It happens to be smaller on the one HWP the roadmap quoted, which is precisely how a single-document measurement misleads.

    **And the tree is DOWNSTREAM, not upstream.** `fastdoc_read_office_tree` calls `read_office` first and builds the tree FROM the `OfficeReadResult` it returns (`fastdoc-ffi/src/lib.rs:337`). Putting the app on the tree adds a hop rather than removing one: reader → schema-v4 → tree → JSON → a new Swift decoder for 29 payload kinds → `OfficeTextBuilder`.

    **The attribution itself has a trap, and the first pass fell in it.** Counting a document's pictures from schema-v4's `images` map undercounts silently: a picture also reaches the export through a table's own picture fill (`TableFormat.picture_fill`, HWP only — 55 tables in one measured manual) and through a master page's artwork, neither of which is in that map. Measured: 66 entries in `images`, **127 resources** in the tree built from that same result, all 127 with distinct sha256 and non-empty bytes, only 66 carrying a `sourceKey`. That gap read as "the two exports disagree about the document" for one whole round. The tree is the one place every picture is accounted for exactly once, so it is the honest denominator for both columns.

    **So S9 closes `OfficeBlock` the other way the roadmap allows — explicitly isolated, not removed** — and the isolation is now a test rather than a paragraph. `RustEngineBridgeTests.testNoProductionSourceCallsASwiftOfficeReader` walks `Sources/FastDocReader`, strips comments (the readers are cited in prose all over this codebase — `OfficeBlock.swift` alone names `DocxReader.isOn` and `HwpReader.read` half a dozen times to say where a field comes from), and fails naming file and line if a production file calls one. `DocumentTypes.officeReaderType(for:)` — the last place the app still named a reader type — became `zipBackedOfficeExtensions`, a registration set, because picking a reader is no longer a thing the app does. Proven to bite: `if ext.isEmpty { return try DocxReader.read(archive) }` inserted into `DocumentTypes.readOffice` failed the test on `DocumentTypes.swift:151` before the line was removed.

    **The rule: a number is an argument only once you know what it is made of.** "78MB" and "78MB of which 24MB is ours" point at different work — the second says the lever is how pictures travel, not which vocabulary describes them.

113. **A PERFORMANCE CLAIM SURVIVES AS A COUNT, NOT AS A CLOCK — gate the property that bought the speed, not the milliseconds it bought.** S10's job was to put gates under three numbers this roadmap had recorded and never guarded. All three had the same shape: an optimisation whose ANSWERS are identical to the slow version's, so the entire existing suite stays green if someone writes the slow shape back. Only the cost moves, and cost is what no other test here can see.

    A wall-clock budget is the obvious gate and the wrong one on this machine. `CLAUDE.md` already records that this suite is flaky under load — the same commit reported 0, 1 and 3 failures on successive runs at load ~50 — so a timing gate would be either loose enough to miss the regression or tight enough to fail on a busy afternoon. Both were tried on this roadmap already and neither is worth keeping.

    **S3's 61× (`produce` 2,104 ms → 34 ms) is now bytes re-scanned.** The defect was `Utf16Index`'s naive form walking from byte 0 for every span endpoint. `record_scan` counts what each query re-scans, and `doubling_a_document_does_not_quadruple_the_work_of_validating_its_spans` requires the total to grow < 3× when the document doubles. Linear is ~2×; measured with the naive cost restored it is exactly **4.00×** (26,220,000 → 104,920,000 bytes) and the gate fails.

    **S8B's remaining +1.22 ms is now traversals.** The engine resize cost +4.3 ms when it landed, of which the FFI boundary was 0.35 ms and the other 4 ms was two extra walks of the storage (grouping the cells, then building the payload from them). Fusing them into the walk that finds the cells is what took the median to 5.86 ms. `testTheResizeWalksTheStorageOnceRatherThanOncePerLayerItReplaced` counts attribute queries through a counting `NSTextStorage` and requires the resize to stay under 1.5× one walk. Measured with the second pass restored: **2,915 → 5,830** against a one-walk cost of 2,915, and the gate fails.

    **S5C-3's +0.020 ms per draw pass gets no gate, and the reason is the general rule.** It does not scale with the document — the batch is filtered by `sheet.intersects(visibleRect)` and the crossing happens once per draw — so it is that value on any document, and a gate on it would be measuring the FFI call's fixed cost, which is not a property anything can regress into.

    **The corpus gate got its evidence back.** `CorpusRenderProbeTests` reads, builds, lays out and re-solves every office document under three real directories. Its verdict travels one channel and its numbers another, and on a 1,982-document run the numbers were lost to stdout interleaving between xctest and the swift-testing runner — the last captured line was a document path with the other runner's banner spliced into the middle of it, which reads exactly like the trap signature the test's own doc comment describes. It was not a trap; the run had passed. `FMD_RENDER_CORPUS_OUT` now writes the report to a file. Current: **found=2029 rendered=2029 readThrew=0**, 18,776 tables, 1,039,017 cell paragraphs, no crash, 736 s.

    **The rule: when an optimisation leaves the answers unchanged, find the countable thing it changed and gate that.** Bytes re-scanned, walks taken, queries made — all deterministic, all immune to the machine's mood, and each one names the regression in its own failure message.

114. **A CENSUS THAT STOPS AT N DOES NOT REPORT ON N — it reports on a sample chosen by sort order, and sort order is not random.** Found in S10 while collecting the corpus evidence, and the number it produced was the reassuring kind. `office_project_corpus_census.rs` walks four directories, sorts, and runs every document through the production `fastdoc_read_office_json`, tabulating how many took the canonical-tree path and how many fell back to the reader. It reported:

    ```
    total examined: 400
    took tree path (project succeeded): 400
    fell back to reader path: 0
    ```

    Read as "nothing falls back", which is exactly what S4's claim wants to hear. But the walk finds **669** office documents and `files.truncate(400)` dropped 269 of them, silently. And the five documents in this repo's own `testdocs` that DO fall back — `GnBS_IM_20260401.docx`, `사업타당성검토보고서_덕소5B구역.docx`, `카카오톡대화_피고진성호.docx`, `OpenAPI활용가이드_특일정보_v1.4.docx`, `tago-tables.odt` — **all five sort past the cut.** The census excluded precisely the cases it exists to count.

    It was found by accident: a different measurement (invariant 112's payload census, which walks `testdocs` and has no cap) showed those five refusing the tree, which could not be reconciled with "0 fallbacks" until the cut was checked.

    **Four censuses had the same cap on the same walk** — `office_project_corpus_census`, `office_corpus_census`, `office_unsupported_graphic_census`, `office_section_field_census`. One of them, `office_corpus_census`, had a real reason (reproducing a leader's `ok=385 / failed=15` baseline over the same 400) and that reason expired the moment `CLAUDE.md` started citing it as the authority on how many documents the tree accepts. A cap that was a deliberate choice for one measurement becomes an unread scope by the next sprint, the same way `-p fastdoc-engine` did (invariant 111).

    All four now walk the whole corpus by default, take a cap only when asked (`FMD_*_CENSUS_LIMIT`), and PRINT what a cap dropped, so no run can read as complete when it was not.

    **The rule: a truncated population must announce its truncation in the same breath as its counts.** "400 examined, 0 fell back" and "400 of 669 examined, 0 fell back" are different claims, and only the second one is honest about being unable to support the first.

115. **THE CANONICAL TREE CANNOT CARRY A ZIP DOCUMENT'S PICTURES, AND THAT IS THE LAZY-MEDIA DESIGN RATHER THAN A HOLE.** Measured in S10, after invariant 114 uncapped the census and the fallback rate went from a reassuring 0 to a real 13 of 669. Eleven of those thirteen refuse as `MissingResource`, and every one is a docx or an odt.

    **No production call site has ever built a non-empty `resources` map.** `fastdoc_read_office_json`, `fastdoc_read_office_tree` and every other caller pass `BTreeMap::new()`. HWP is unaffected because its pictures ride inside `OfficeReadResult.images` (rhwp pre-decodes them), so the adapter finds them; a docx or odt keeps its pictures in the archive and the reader emits only the relationship target (`word/media/image1.png`), so `resolve_resource` asks for a key nothing supplied.

    **Filling the map would be a first-paint regression, not a fix.** `office_project.rs:818` decodes each resource and inserts its bytes into the projected `result.images`, so a docx that today returns a payload with NO picture bytes — measured: a 10.2 MB docx exports 922,833 bytes, a 1.8 MB one exports 6,943 — would start returning every picture inline, at open time, on the main path. That is exactly the eager-media shape the host's lazy `reconcileMedia` exists to avoid, and it is the same lever invariant 112 names: pictures are two thirds of what a payload weighs.

    So the fallback is doing its job for those eleven, and the numbers to keep in mind are:

    | | of 669 real documents |
    |---|---|
    | took the canonical-tree path | 656 → **658** after the tab-stop fix below |
    | fell back to the reader path | 13 → **11**, all `MissingResource` (docx/odt with pictures) |
    | `read_office` returned NULL | 0 |

    Re-measured after the fix: `ok=658 failed=11`, `Canonicalization` gone. The two censuses agree
    exactly, which is worth having — they call DIFFERENT exported symbols
    (`fastdoc_read_office_tree` and `fastdoc_read_office_json`) and arrive at the same 669/658/11.

    **The two `Canonicalization` refusals were a real defect, and the census is what surfaced them.**
    That variant's own doc comment says it "should never happen for a correctly implemented adapter",
    and it was happening on two real documents: `Invariant("tab stops are not finite strictly
    increasing positions")`. `sanitize_tab_stops` sorted and stopped there, while the validator
    demands STRICTLY increasing — so a paragraph declaring `[30.0, 30.0, 30.0, 30.0]`, all Left, all
    with the same leader (measured: 2 of the 21 paragraphs in `rowbreak-problem-pages.hwp` that carry
    stops at all) sorted cleanly and refused the whole document. A tab stop list is a set of
    positions: a document naming one position four times has said one thing four times. Duplicates
    now collapse first-declared-wins, and NaN/infinite/negative positions are dropped rather than
    carried into a tree that would be refused entire. Both documents take the tree path now.

    **What this costs is validation, not correctness.** The output is identical either way — `project` round-trips (invariant 112's oracle) and `to_json(&result)` is the same shape — but a document that falls back never passes through the tree's validation. So S4's claim reads more broadly than it holds: every shipping payload is derived from a validated tree *unless the document keeps its pictures in its own archive*, which no test said out loud until this census could see all 669 documents.

    **The rule: when a refusal is the design, say so where the refusal is counted.** A `MissingResource` that no caller can avoid is not a defect waiting to be fixed, and leaving it unexplained invites someone to "fix" it by populating the map — which would trade a validation gap for a memory and first-paint one.

116. **THE PORT'S ONLY END-TO-END PARITY CHECK NEEDS A BINARY FROM BEFORE THE CUTOVER, AND THAT BINARY IS NOT REPRODUCIBLE FROM THIS BRANCH.** S10's parity axis is `extract_matches_swift.rs`: it drives real `.docx`/`.odt` through this engine's `--extract` and compares the bytes against an installed FastDocReader's `--extract` for the same file. Its own doc calls this "the only check in the workspace that can tell a faithful port from a plausible one", and it is right — nothing else in either suite reads a real document with both implementations.

    **What it compares changed under it when S9 landed, silently.** The app no longer reads office documents with the Swift readers; it links this engine. Point `FMD_EXTRACT_SWIFT` at a CURRENT build and only the serializer half is still independent (the host keeps `OfficeMarkdownSerializer.swift`, `HeadlessExtract.swift:82`) — the reader half compares the engine with itself and passes for the wrong reason. Point it at a build from before the cutover and both halves are independent, which is the check as written.

    S10's run used the installed `2026-08-19` build, the last one that shipped the Swift readers: **574 real documents, 566 byte-identical, 8 refused by both, 0 differing.** The repo's own corpus separately: 21 of 21 identical.

    **The baseline is a one-way door.** That binary cannot be rebuilt from this branch — the readers it used are no longer wired to anything a build produces. Keep a copy of a pre-cutover build if this comparison is ever wanted again; a fresh `make-app.sh` will not give you one, and the test cannot tell you it has stopped comparing two things.

    **The rule: a differential test names one implementation by a path, not by a property, so it cannot notice when that path stops being the other implementation.** Say in the test what the baseline must be, and record which build produced the numbers.

117. **A WALL-CLOCK BASELINE IS ONLY A BASELINE WHILE IT IS RE-MEASURED BESIDE THE THING IT IS COMPARED TO — a number from a quieter hour reads as a regression that was never there.** Found in P4c, at the point the performance-recovery run stopped to check its own work. The plan's opening measurement recorded `first_paint` at **526 ms**; the integrated re-measure after P0–P4c reported **924.4 ms**, and the natural reading was that work had simply moved out of `read_parse` (2,966 → 1,375.5) and into the paint. That reading would have been acted on: the next step was to prove P2c's lazy pictures were not being fetched for the whole document at first paint.

    Two things were done instead of reasoning about it. First the hypothesis was **killed by code** — `reconcileMedia` gathers `officeLoad` only inside `if onScreen(r)`, every call site is behind `DispatchQueue.main.async`, and the probe stops its timer before `spin(2)`, so no picture fetch can land in that stage. Second, the base commit `117f388` was checked out into a temporary worktree, its engine built there, and the SAME probe run on the SAME machine within the hour:

    | | plan's recorded baseline | re-measured, same day | after P0–P4c |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | read_parse | 2,966 | **3,771.7** | 1,375.5 |
    | first_paint | 526 | **895.3** | 924.4 |
    | scroll median | 48.8 | **60.6** | 50.6 |
    | footprint after readthrough | 475 | **521.2** | 427.9 |

    **`first_paint` had not regressed at all** — 924.4 against a true baseline of 895.3 is +3.3%, inside this machine's spread. And the error ran in BOTH directions: scroll looked flat (48.8 → 50.6) when it had improved 16.5%, and memory after a readthrough looked worse when it had improved 17.9%.

    Why the first numbers were lower is `[미확인]`, and deliberately left that way rather than guessed at: same day, same command, same release configuration. `CLAUDE.md` already records that this suite is flaky under load — a quiet machine finishes it in ~14 s and a busy one in 40–55 s — and this probe reads the wall clock straight through that. The old figures are not called wrong; they are dropped from the comparison, because a number whose conditions cannot be reconstructed cannot be compared against.

    **The rule: gates count (invariant 113), but the wall-clock numbers a REPORT carries need the same discipline in a weaker form — re-measure the baseline beside the new build, on the same machine, in the same hour, or say the comparison is not one.** The cost of the rule is one worktree and one probe run. The cost of skipping it was nearly a night spent hunting a 400 ms cause that did not exist.

118. **A SCROLLED FRAME'S COST IS THIS APP'S OWN DRAW CODE, NOT TEXTKIT — and on a paged Korean document almost all of it was the 바탕쪽's artwork being resampled sixty times a second.** P5 set out to put a gate under the scroll number the roadmap had carried unguarded (median 50.6 ms, worst 140.8) and expected the cause to be layout or glyph generation. It was neither, and the measurement that settled it took one line: returning from `ReaderTextView.drawBackground` immediately after `super`.

    | what was drawn | median viewport |
    |---|---|
    | everything | 51.3 ms |
    | nothing of ours | **2.1 ms** |
    | + page sheets | 5.2 |
    | + `drawMasterPages` | **58.2** |

    Inside that: the master-page batch ~51 ms, anchored objects 2.7. Inside the batch, one `case` skipped at a time — `.image` alone 16.0, `.text` alone 48.5, `.vector` alone 46.8. **The artwork.**

    **The cost is the RESAMPLE, not the decode.** Wrapping the decoded `CGImage` in a fresh `NSImage` took 51.3 → 48.6. Caching the artwork already scaled to the size it is DRAWN at — magnification in the key, so a zoom re-renders instead of stretching — took it to **41.2**.

    Three things this cost, recorded so they are not re-earned:

    - **Printing must keep the original.** A bitmap sized for a 395pt screen sheet in a print context puts a screen-resolution picture into the PDF, which `--pdf` reports no error for. The predicate is `NSPrintOperation.current`, NOT `currentContextDrawingToScreen()`: `cacheDisplay(in:to:)` is not "the screen", and it is how both the reader's probe and the gate make a draw provably happen, so the obvious predicate turns the cache off in exactly the measurements that judge it. Verified: the reference document still prints to 17,341,890 bytes with 131 image XObjects.
    - **`ObjectIdentifier` is an address.** Key a cache by it and a freed original lets the next `NSImage` inherit the same one, so the cache answers with a picture from a document that is no longer open. The gate found this itself, reading zero rasterisations on a test whose artwork had been freed. The entry holds the source alongside the scaled copy, which makes the address unrecyclable while the key can still be found.
    - **Rasterising only the part that lands on the paper buys nothing.** An artwork can be bigger than its sheet (2652×1940 device pixels was measured) and the clip hides the result without stopping the blit, so cutting the cached bitmap down to the sheet looked obvious. Measured: 40.8 vs 41.2 — inside the noise. It was reverted. The remaining ~30 ms is the blit of an already-correctly-sized bitmap (`.image` skipped entirely is 10.8 ms), and neither caching nor clipping touches it; the next lever is not redrawing unchanged page furniture at all.

    **The rule: before optimising a draw, find out how much of the frame is yours.** One early return answered it here, and it pointed at a feature nobody suspected instead of at the text engine everybody would have.

119. **A TEST THAT WRITES A PREFERENCE HAS CHANGED THE APP, NOT ITS OWN FIXTURE — and the measurement that runs afterwards reports the change as an optimisation.** Found in P8's integrated re-measure, by a number that was too good. `PageGridMemoTests` set the page-outline preference in `setUp` and left it OFF in `tearDown`, which looked like tidy cleanup. `PageViewOptionsStore.current` is backed by `UserDefaults.standard`, so that write outlives the test, the class, and the process: every later test in the run saw a reader with the page outline off, and so did `ReaderPerfProbeTests`.

    It reported a **2.2 ms** median viewport against 41 ms, and **713** read-through steps against 957. Read alone, that is a twenty-fold win. What it actually was is a document with no pages drawn on it — no sheets, no 바탕쪽, no page band — which is also why the document got shorter. The tell was the step count, not the milliseconds: a faster reader does not have less document.

    Two things follow, and only the first is about tests.

    - **Restore what you found, never write "the default".** The value belongs to whoever set it. `PageViewOptionsStore.intent` is the unresolved choice and is what a `tearDown` must put back. Writing `PageViewOptions(outline: false)` is not cleanup, it is a decision about someone else's preference, and on a developer's machine it reaches the app.
    - **A preference read by a probe is an input to the measurement.** Nothing in the probe's output says which page options were in force, so two runs of the same commit can differ by twenty times with no visible cause. Anything that changes what a probe measures has to be either fixed by the probe or printed by it; on a shared `UserDefaults` domain (`com.apple.dt.xctest.tool` here) it is neither by default.

    The same session had already learned the general form of this the expensive way (invariant 117): a number is only comparable to another number taken under the same conditions, and "the same commit" is not the same conditions.

120. **A BYTE-IDENTITY AXIS THAT DOES NOT RECORD ITS PRODUCER IS NOT RE-CHECKABLE — and the 306 bytes it left behind cost three clean builds to close.** P2c's regression table recorded `--extract 612,472 = 612,472, cmp IDENTICAL` on the reference document. P4a, measuring the same axis, got **612,778** and could not reproduce the earlier figure from the immediately preceding commit either, so the sprint carried it as `[미확인]` — a possible 306 bytes of lost content on the one oracle that says a document still comes out the same.

    It was not a regression. Three release builds made from clean checkouts — P2c's parent `191863a`, P2c itself `f2cc19d`, and `HEAD` — each produce **612,778 bytes, `cmp`-identical to each other**, and that is also the number `p0-baseline.md` recorded for the engine before any of this work started. No document in `testdocs` over 2 MB produces 612,472. **Nothing was lost; the record was wrong.**

    **The relink is not the culprit, and that is worth knowing on its own.** The obvious suspect was the app failing to pick up a rebuilt engine, which would make any in-session before/after compare a binary against itself. Tested rather than assumed, by mutation: `Span.text` in `office_project.rs` was changed to append one character per run, and the ordinary `build-engine.sh` → `make-app.sh release` sequence carried it through to a different binary and **612,778 → 622,539** in the app's own `--extract`. The pipeline propagates. Restored, `diff`-checked, and the worktree deleted.

    What remains unexplained is only which producer emitted 612,472 in that session — and that is exactly the gap the rule closes. **A row asserting byte identity records the SHA of the binary that produced each side, next to the number.** Without it the row cannot be audited later: "identical" may well have been true of whatever it compared, but nothing in the record says what that was, and a figure no build of that source has ever produced reads for months afterwards as a defect in the app rather than a defect in the notebook.


    **Two bundles do not share a preference, and nothing in their output says so.** Comparing the shipped build against the preserved pre-cutover one (2026-08-30) the identifiers differ — `ai.ww-w.fast-md-reader` against `…dev` — so each reads its own `baseFontSize`: **17 in one, 10 in the other**. `--extract` never lays anything out and is immune, which is why `Scripts/perf-baseline.sh` measures only that. `--pdf` is not: an UNPAGED document paginates by the reading size alone, and the same markdown file printed **16 pages from one build and 9 from the other**, which reads as a rendering regression and is a preference. Equalised, 8 of 9 formats produce the identical page count. A paged document mostly escapes it (invariant 46 — a paged press is a view transform), which is what makes the trap quiet: the office formats agree, so the one that does not looks like a real defect.
121. **A BLIT WHOSE DESTINATION IS NOT THE BITMAP'S OWN PIXEL COUNT IS NOT A BLIT — CoreGraphics resamples the whole picture, and on a 바탕쪽 that was half the remaining scroll cost.** Invariant 118 cached the artwork at the size it is DRAWN at and took the median viewport from 51.3 ms to 41.0. The 30 ms that remained was assumed to be the composite itself and written down as needing a structural change — layer backing, dirty rectangles. It was not. It was one rounding.

    The cached copy is a whole number of pixels; the rectangle it lands in is not. Measured on the reference document: a **1111-pixel** bitmap drawn into **1111.18** device pixels. Timed offscreen on that artwork, 1.69 megapixels, medians of 40 draws:

    | how it is drawn | median |
    |---|---|
    | `NSImage.draw`, `.sourceOver`, point space | 17.5 ms |
    | `NSImage.draw`, `.copy` | 19.2 |
    | opaque copy (alpha byte skipped) | 17.8 |
    | `CGContext.draw`, point space | 16.0 |
    | destination snapped to whole POINTS | 16.6 |
    | **device space, one-to-one** | **7.2** |
    | device space, one-to-one, HIGH interpolation | **7.25** |

    Two things fall out of the last two rows. **Blending, the alpha channel and `NSImage`'s own overhead are not where the time is** — the first four rows are one number. And **the interpolation FLAG is not the lever either**: at one-to-one, high quality costs 0.05 ms, because CoreGraphics has nothing to interpolate. Turning interpolation down in point space (7.2 ms) buys the same as aligning does, which is the tell — both are the same saving, arrived at from opposite ends, and only alignment keeps the picture correct at every other zoom.

    So `MasterPagePainter.drawArtwork` undoes the view's transform, rounds the destination's origin to a whole device pixel, and draws the `CGImage` in a rectangle that is exactly its own pixel count. The artwork can move by half a device pixel; nothing else changes. In device space the bitmap is upright by CG's own convention, so the flipped-view correction (`respectFlipped:`) is gone rather than restated.

    **Paired measurement, two rounds, alternating builds in one session on a loaded machine (invariant 117 — the recorded 41.0 was taken on a quiet one and is not comparable): median scrolled viewport 72.2 / 72.3 ms before, 40.1 / 40.0 after; the whole read-through 71.6 s to 46.1 s.** Printing is untouched: `NSPrintOperation.current` still hands paper the original.

    **The gate reads the destination rectangle, not a clock.** A copy and a resample put identical pixels on the screen, so nothing else in the suite can see this (invariant 113). `lastArtworkBlit` records what the last blit actually asked for, and the test asserts its size equals the bitmap's pixel count and its origin is integral. Proven to bite — three value substitutions, each restored and `diff`-checked: an unrounded origin (2 failures), a size taken from the fractional rectangle (2), and falling back to the point-space draw (6).

    A second gate reads the PIXELS, because orientation is the one thing a rectangle cannot say: in device space a `CGImage` is upright by CoreGraphics' own convention, and every other check here passes perfectly on a page whose 바탕쪽 is upside down. It draws a picture that is red along the top and blue along the bottom and reads both back. Mirroring the draw about its own centre fails it (2 failures).

    **The cache's own ceiling was re-measured afterwards, because this changed what a frame costs and invariant 117 applies to it too.** Paired, two rounds each: 24 MB gives 40.1 / 39.4 ms and 466.6 / 430.8 MB after a full read-through; 16 MB gives 51.5 / 51.2 ms and 417.5 / 420.3 MB. The bound is worth **11.6 ms a frame for about 30 MB** — a better trade than the +90 MB the sprint had carried, which was an estimate against the 64-ENTRY version rather than a measurement of one ceiling against the other. 24 MB stays.

122. **A `SwiftString` that rebuilds its UTF-16 index on every read is not an index — random access is the one reason the type exists, and the transliterated markdown renderer paid 20.2 MINUTES for it.** `nsstring.rs`'s own module note quotes `MarkdownRenderer.swift:172-179`: the reader casts to `NSString` "to get UTF-16 random access cheaply". The shim then implemented every UTF-16 accessor as `self.s.encode_utf16().collect()` — the whole document, per call. `AttributedBuilder::new` scans for line starts one character at a time, so a document cost O(n) per character.

    Measured on the restored transliteration, release both sides, `demo/moby-dick.md` (1,224,921 characters):

    | | parse | render |
    |---|---|---|
    | host (Swift, `swift-markdown`) | 44.8 ms | **478.8 ms** |
    | engine, before | 10.3 | **1,214,640.8** |
    | engine, after | 7.2 | **120.5** |

    The shape was confirmed before anything was changed, across a 128x size range — `time / chars²` held at 9.8e-7 · 7.7e-7 · 7.1e-7 · 8.1e-7 for 9.5K, 17.6K, 284.7K and 1.22M characters. **The first fix was aimed at the wrong line.** `NSAttributedString::length()` had the same shape (`encode_utf16().count()` per call, with `append` calling it first), it is genuinely quadratic, and fixing it changed the total by 1.3% — because it was not the one being called per character. `sample(1)` on the test binary named the real one immediately: **15,363 of 15,376 stacks** were `AttributedBuilder::new → SwiftString::characterAt → Vec::from_iter`. Profile before choosing, or the honest fix lands on the honest wrong line.

    The index is **lazy, not eager**: `SwiftString` is a field of 62 wire and schema types that never index anything, and materialising UTF-16 for all of them would roughly double the engine's string memory — which P2 and P4 had just finished halving. It is built on first indexed access and dropped by `push_str`, the only place the text changes, so it cannot go stale. `length()` deliberately still COUNTS rather than reading the index, so asking a never-indexed string its length does not make it hold a UTF-16 copy for good. `Eq`/`Ord`/`Hash` are hand-written over the text alone so "has been indexed" is not observable, and `Clone` starts the copy un-indexed.

    **The gate counts index builds, not milliseconds.** `swiftshim::nsstring::utf16_index_builds()` is a process-wide counter; the test renders a document and the same document doubled, and asserts the count does not grow with it. A count is immune to machine load, which matters here because this repo's own suite names its wall-clock tests as repeat false-failure offenders. Proven to bite: removing the cache reports **35,601 builds for 29,400 characters and 71,201 for 58,800** — exactly proportional, failing outright rather than by a margin.

123. **A UTF-16 offset indexing a UTF-8 string is correct for ASCII and panics mid-character on the first Korean document.** `visitBlockQuote` collapses trailing blank lines by reading the last two positions through `ns.substring(with: NSRange(...))` — UTF-16 units, because everything in that file is. The transliteration wrote those same offsets into a Rust byte slice: `self.result.string()[self.result.length() - 1..]`. Every existing shape test passed, because every fixture was ASCII. A fixture with one Hangul line fails as `byte index 1242 is not a char boundary; it is inside '한' (bytes 1240..1243)`.

    The fix asks the same question without an index at all — `\n` is one UTF-16 unit and one UTF-8 byte and can never occur inside a multi-byte sequence, so `ends_with("\n\n")` is byte-for-byte the original's test. Restoring the byte-index form fails the suite (1 failure), so it is held.

    **The rule this leaves: a transliteration's fixtures must contain a character outside ASCII.** The mis-port is invisible otherwise, and this is a Korean-document reader.

124. **On this machine most of a 15-minute engine gate is macOS evaluating signatures, not tests running.** The workspace `cargo test` was treated for the whole migration as "large suite, takes a while". Split: compile **150 s**, execution **1,019 s** — of which the process itself used **66 s of user CPU**, i.e. **93% of the execution window was spent waiting**. The hottest process on the machine during it was `syspolicyd` at 53.9%.

    The unit of cost is the test BINARY, not the test. Each `tests/*.rs` is its own executable (43 of them here) and each is freshly linked whenever its crate changes, so each is a new unsigned executable that Gatekeeper assesses once. Same binary, first run **8.37 s**; second run **0.00 s**; a rebuilt one, 8-plus again.

    Two consequences. **Narrowing the run does not buy what it looks like it buys** — halving the tests does not halve the wait unless it halves the BINARIES, and invariant 111 already forbids narrowing by crate. And `target/` accumulates hash-suffixed copies of old binaries: 231 executables, 15 GB, which is a measurement hazard rather than a build one (deleting the stale ones did not trigger a recompile — `cargo build --tests` stayed a 6.5 s no-op). The lever that fits the measurement is exempting the terminal that spawns the tests under **System Settings → Privacy & Security → Developer Tools**; `spctl developer-mode enable-terminal` adds Terminal.app specifically, which is the wrong app if the tests run under any other terminal. **Not yet measured after the change** — the number above is the cost, not the saving.

125. **`--pdf` refuses to overwrite, so a comparison loop that does not delete its output measures the PREVIOUS document — and two builds do not share a preference domain.** Both traps were hit in one sitting while answering "why does the print differ between the pre-cutover build and this one", and each produced a confident wrong answer first.

    The output file: `--pdf` writes only if the path is free (`-f` forces). A loop that reuses one path silently keeps the first document's PDF, and every later row of the table repeats it. It reads as "the setting has no effect" — the page count and even the per-size font scales were byte-identical across three pinned sizes, which is exactly what a real invariance looks like. What gave it away was the counts being identical for a DIFFERENT document. **Delete the output, or pass `-f`, and put a value in the table that must change between rows.**

    The domain: the shipped bundle is `ai.ww-w.fast-md-reader`; `make-app.sh` builds `ai.ww-w.fast-md-reader.dev`. They are different `UserDefaults` domains, so `defaults write` against one leaves the other alone — and reading the SOURCE `Resources/Info.plist` shows only the shipped id, which is how the difference gets missed. Read the identifier out of each built bundle.

    **Markdown and office differ in whether any of this matters.** An office document's column is pinned to its own page body (invariant 57), so its pagination is comparable across builds. Markdown wraps at the reading size, and the two builds resolved that to **17 pt and 10 pt** — a 1.7x ratio visible in every text run (body 17.0/10.0, code 15.3/9.0, headings 25.5/15.0 and 31.88/18.75) and worth 9 pages against 4 on the same file. **A markdown page count is not a like-for-like comparison between two builds until that size is proven equal on both**, and neither build moved when the preference was pinned in either domain, so what actually determines it headlessly is `[unverified]`.

126. **The engine build paginates a real Word document one page CLOSER to Word than the reader it replaced, and its print cost moves in both directions.** Chasing "the port lost a page" found the opposite. On `OpenAPI활용가이드_특일정보_v1.4.docx` the pre-cutover build printed **21** pages and this one prints **20**; the file's own `docProps/app.xml`, written by Word 16, records **19**. The divergence is on page 1, where the older build pushed the first table to page 2 and this one fits it — so the remaining one-page gap to Word is a fidelity question, not a migration regression.

    Print time is **not** a uniform regression either, minimum of three runs each, quiet machine: that docx goes 863 → 992 ms (**+15.0%**), while `giant-table.odt` goes 5,369 → 4,432 ms (**−17.5%**). The transport probe attributes 37.0 ms of engine FFI plus 32.3 ms of host decode on the docx — **69 ms against a 129 ms difference, so transport explains about half and the rest is `[unverified]`.** Quoting a single document's print delta as the port's cost is what produced the "+15%" headline; it is one document's number.

127. **An UNPAGED document prints through `.fit`, so whatever width its text view happens to have decides the printed type size — and headless, that width was a constant nobody could see.** Invariant 59 pins a PAGED document's paper to its own sheet; markdown and plain text have no sheet, so `makePrintOperation` falls through to `horizontalPagination = .fit`, which scales the text view onto the printer's imageable width. The scale is therefore `imageable ÷ viewWidth`, and nothing in that expression is a property of the document.

    Measured, same file (`demo/moby-dick.md`), only the off-screen seed changed: **820 pt → 147 pages, 1640 pt → 46 pages.** The same mechanism explains a difference between two builds that looked like a font-size regression: body type came out at 17 pt in one and 10 pt in the other, an exact 1.7x across every size class (code 15.3/9.0, headings 25.5/15.0 and 31.88/18.75) — but the stored `baseFontSize` was **16** in both, so it was never the font size. Back-solving `imageable ÷ scale` puts the two text views at roughly 440 pt and 749 pt for the same 820 pt window; only one of the builds was propagating the frame into the view. **A page count is not evidence of a pagination change until the print scale is known to be 1.**

    `--pdf` now takes the seed back out: after the first settle it corrects the window so the text view lands ON the paper column (`HeadlessPDF.printColumnCorrection`, one step because window-to-view is linear), then re-settles, so `.fit` has nothing left to scale and the file prints at the size it is read at. A paged document is deliberately excluded — its paper is its page body, and moving the window would only make the reader disagree with the sheet. The gate varies the seed (820 against 1640) through a test-only parameter on `run` and asserts an EQUAL page count; mutation-checked, removing the correction reports **13 against 7**.

    **Interactive ⌘P is untouched and still window-dependent** — a wide window prints small type, a narrow one prints large. That is the same defect on the path a person uses, and fixing it means reflowing the reader's own column to the paper and back, which changes every attachment size that was measured against the reading column. Left as a decision, not silently half-fixed.

128. **The markdown engine's win survives a real font world — and the thing standing between it and the app is a tree CONSUMER nobody has written, not a measurement.** M1's engine numbers were taken inside `cargo test`, where the font world is one synthetic face and nothing is ever substituted: a floor, and said so. `MarkdownEngineParityProbeTests` closes that by driving `fastdoc_read_text_tree` from the HOST with the CoreText-backed provider installed, so both arms pay for the same fonts, the same substitutions and the same machine. Release, `demo/moby-dick.md` (1,224,921 characters), minimum of three:

    | arm | ms |
    |---|---|
    | host — `MarkdownRenderer` source → `NSAttributedString` | **410.7** |
    | engine — source → canonical tree JSON | **44.3** |
    | + host decode FLOOR — one `JSONSerialization` scan of that 7.2 MB envelope | **48.3** |

    **The third row is the row that keeps this honest.** The engine arm stops at JSON; the host arm ends at a laid-out attributed string, so quoting 44.3 against 410.7 would be the same error as quoting one document's print delta as a port's cost (invariant 126). The comparable figure is the engine plus its transport — **92.6 ms against 410.7 ms** — and even that is a floor, because a real consumer decodes into typed values and builds typography where this only scans bytes once.

    So the win is real (about 4x, transport included) and it is still not wired, deliberately. `RustCanonicalTree.swift` lifts `schemaVersion` and keeps the rest as opaque bytes, saying full typing "is S6's job"; **it has no callers anywhere in the app.** The tree it would have to type is 1,116 lines of wire schema inside 9,012 lines of render-tree code, and the consumer would then have to reach parity with the whole markdown renderer — code cards, mermaid and KaTeX blocks, tables, attachments, links, footnotes. That is a sprint. Half of it, landed on the format the app is named for, is worse than none of it, so what exists here is the measurement that makes the decision answerable and nothing that pretends to be the decision.

129. **P4b: the repeated `edge_borders` object was a fifth of the host's decode, and the estimate that deferred it was wrong in the safe direction.** P4a pooled the pictures; the roadmap then parked `format` and `edge_borders` because the case for them rested on "about 300 ms" derived from an average, with a worry attached — Foundation opens a container per paragraph either way, so pooling might recover only half. **Neither half of that guess survived measurement.**

    The ceiling was measured directly, not modelled: dump a real payload (`FMD_PAYLOAD_DUMP` on the `payload_composition` harness), delete the field from the JSON, and decode both through the SHIPPING path (`PayloadDecodeProbeTests` → `RustEngine.decodeOffice`). Release, the 2,562-page manual, minimum of three, quiet machine:

    | payload | ms |
    |---|---|
    | as the engine writes it | **585.0** |
    | `edge_borders` deleted | **445.5** (−139.5, −23.8%) |
    | `format` deleted | **458.9** (−122.8) |
    | both deleted | **329.6** (−252.1, −43.3%) |
    | `format` reduced to its smallest LEGAL container | **505.0** (−80.0) |

    The last row is the one that answers the worry: the nested container is worth about 43 ms of `format`'s 123, so removing the object entirely recovers half again as much as shrinking it. **The bytes were never the point — 5,494 nested containers were.**

    Only `edge_borders` shipped. It was already `Option` on both sides, so it needed no schema change, and the host mechanism already existed: a task-local bound in `RustEngine.decodeOffice` before the body decodes, exactly as `PictureBytes` does, because the value has been folded into an `OfficeBlock` enum case before any post-pass could run. Measured on the same manual: payload **13,181,914 → 11,516,320 (−12.6%)**, 5,494 inline declarations → **0**, pool **274 entries** — the distinct count predicted by the composition table, exactly.

    **The decode gain must be read from a PAIRED run, and this is where a wrong answer nearly shipped.** Measured alone after the change it read 791.9 ms against the 585.0 ms recorded earlier and looked like a 35% REGRESSION; the machine had simply gone from idle to load 18.9 in between. Alternating the two payloads on one build in one session, twice: **994.9 / 982.8 ms inline against 785.9 / 788.4 ms pooled, −20%** — most of the −23.8% ceiling, with the rest being the pool's own decode. Invariant 67 already recorded this exact trap for attribute hashing; it cost an hour again here. **Never compare a number taken now against one written down earlier.**

    **The same trap caught a second number in the same hour.** The regression axes record the reference HWP at 542 pages; this build prints **537**, which read as a five-page pagination regression from the wire change. Building the two preceding commits on this machine and printing the same file gives **537 on all three** — `e6d2794`, `2f5932a` and this one — so the pooling changed nothing and 542 is simply older than the session that wrote it down. Its origin is `[unverified]`; what is verified is that nothing in this session moved it. `--extract` is byte-identical at 612,778 across the change, which is the axis that would have caught real content loss.

    `format` is deliberately NOT done: it is a required field on four block variants, so pooling it means making the wire's `format` optional across the engine and the host — the schema change the roadmap correctly identified as bigger than P4a. Its ceiling is now a measurement (−123 ms whole, −80 ms as a shrunken container) instead of an average, so the decision no longer rests on a guess.

130. **Markdown crosses to the host as a finished attributed string, and the wire names fonts and colours by the ROLE that made them — because neither a system font nor a dynamic colour survives being sent as a value.** Markdown is 86% typography and 14% parse (measured: 97.8–99.1 ms parsing against 708–714 ms total), so the office precedent — send the STRUCTURE and let the host build it — recovers almost nothing here. `fastdoc_render_markdown` therefore sends what `MarkdownRenderer` would have produced: the text, plus the `addAttribute` calls that decorated it.

    **The layers are a call LOG, not a run list.** `swiftshim`'s `NSAttributedString.runs` is the append-ordered record of every `addAttribute`, and those ranges OVERLAP — a paragraph style across a block, a font over one word inside it. AppKit's own `runs` tile; these do not. A first version of the projection test asserted they tiled and failed immediately, which is the cheapest possible way to learn this. The host replays them in order, later calls winning.

    **Two things cannot be sent as values, and both look correct until someone changes the appearance or reads a script.**

    | sent as | what breaks |
    |---|---|
    | the resolved FACE | `NSFont.systemFont(ofSize:weight:)` is the private `.AppleSystemUIFontDemi` cascade; feeding its own descriptor back to `NSFont(descriptor:size:)` yields the concrete `.SFNS-Semibold` — different metrics (6.33 against 6.41 advance at 30pt) and no cascade to find a Hangul glyph |
    | the resolved COMPONENTS | every palette colour is a light/dark dynamic, and the engine has no appearance to resolve one against (`swiftshim`'s documented phase-A `DynamicColor::resolve` keeps the light half), so the whole document renders light-mode forever |

    So the wire carries `role` — `body` · `heading(level)` · `code` · `tableHeader` for fonts, `text` · `secondary` · `link` · `inlineCode` · the five highlighter system colours · `secondaryLabel` for colours — and the host rebuilds each with the SAME AppKit call the renderer would have made. The role is recovered at projection time by asking the theme what each of its roles resolves to and keying a table on the answer, which is exact because markdown only ever paints with theme values. `raw` is the escape hatch, and `testNothingFallsThroughToTheRawEscapeHatch` holds it at **zero** across the five shipped documents — a `raw` value is not an error, it is the signal that a role is missing.

    **Substitution belongs to the host, exactly as it does on the office path.** Run on both sides it happens twice: the engine resolves Hangul through the provider bridge to one face and the host re-substitutes what arrives to another (`.AppleKoreanFont-Bold` against the `.AppleSDGothicNeoI-SemiBold` this reader draws). `render_before_host_font_substitution` is the form that crosses; leaving substitution undone is also what keeps every font in the string a THEME font, which is what makes the roles work at all.

    **The parity oracle earned itself four times over.** `MarkdownEngineParityTests` compares the two finished strings attribute by attribute (paragraph styles field by field, table identity by slot-number agreement rather than by pointer, since neither side's pointers mean anything to the other). Building it surfaced four defects that no structural test could see, three of them PRE-EXISTING and one of them shipping today:

    - **Smart punctuation was off.** swift-markdown parses through cmark-gfm with it on, so this reader has always shown `’`; comrak's `parse.smart` defaulted off and the engine produced `'` on all five shipped documents. The tree was identical; only leaf text differed.
    - **A markdown table cell dropped a link whose text was inline code** — `[`code-blocks.md`](code-blocks.md)`, which is how `demo/README.md` writes every row. Grafting the table block onto the cell's paragraph style rebuilt the run's attributes from `attributesAt`, which returns ONE layer's dictionary rather than the merged set, and `setAttributes` then retired every layer at that exact range. The inline-code attributes and the link attributes sit on the identical range, so the link and its underline were deleted. Fixed with `replace_attribute_value`, which edits the layer that already carries the key and leaves its siblings alone. **`TableBlockBuilder` is shared with the office readers, so this was a live defect for any office table cell with a link in it.**
    - **A cell terminator lost its paragraph style.** AppKit's `enumerateAttribute` visits the whole range and hands `nil` for the stretches with no value; this shim's walks the layers it has and never reports a gap. The Swift original relies on the `nil` visits to give an un-styled terminator a fresh style carrying the table block. The gaps are now filled explicitly in the builder.
    - **`NSTextAlignment::default()` was `Left`.** AppKit's is `.natural`. A paragraph the renderer never aligns explicitly came out left-aligned, which is invisible in English and wrong in every right-to-left document.

    Enum raw values are named in the wire rather than cast from the shim's declaration order (`appkit_alignment`, `appkit_writing_direction`) — the orders agree for alignment and do NOT for writing direction, where AppKit numbers `natural` as −1.

    The reader now renders markdown through `RustMarkdownEngine.renderOrHost`, which falls back to `MarkdownRenderer` when the engine returns nil. That is not defensive padding: the engine ships as a PREBUILT library, so "installed engine older than this app" is a real state, and the right answer to it is to open the document. One path is deliberately NOT wired — front-first progressive paint, which hands out a piece at a time and would need a wire per chunk plus block-id and source-offset continuity across them.

    **A table is pooled by IDENTITY, and getting that wrong was the one defect the parity oracle could not see.** Every markdown table declares the same thing — n columns, borders collapsed — so interning them by value merged all of them into one: a document of 60 tables produced **1**, and AppKit laid all sixty out as a single grid. The parity corpus never caught it because no document in it had two tables; `PrecomputeLayoutTests.testAStepIsBoundedByCharactersNotByStructure` did, because it counts distinct tables as its own premise. `swiftshim`'s `NSTextTable` is a VALUE cloned into each cell, so it had no identity to pool by at all — it now carries a clone-stable `id` (deliberately outside `PartialEq`, which still compares what a table declares), and `Pool::intern_by` keys on that. `testEveryTableInADocumentStaysItsOwnTable` is the gate that was missing.

    Office is untouched by all of this, and that is measured rather than argued: the same HWP through the pre-change and post-change builds, alternating in one session, gives **42 pages / 16,925,925-byte PDF / 45,656-byte `--extract` on both**.

131. **Front-first paint goes on the engine as a HANDLE, not as one call per chunk — and the handle's whole contract is that a failed chunk is unrecoverable.** Invariant 130 wired the whole-document render and deliberately left progressive paint on the host, because a piece at a time needs block-id and source-offset continuity across calls. Re-rendering from the top for each chunk is quadratic, and passing the parse tree back and forth per chunk pays the cost invariant 129 exists to avoid. So `fastdoc_markdown_progressive_open/_next/_is_finished/_close` mirrors `fastdoc_office_open`: the parse and the theme live in a `Box`ed handle, `_next` advances it by a block count and returns one wire, and the owner of the document's lifetime holds it and closes it in `deinit`.

    **The unwind claim is narrow and written down rather than assumed.** Each entry point wraps the handle in `AssertUnwindSafe`, which is a claim about what a panic can leave behind. The claim made here is only this: if a chunk panics the handle has advanced by some blocks and is in no other sense damaged, and the ONLY supported response is to close it and fall back to the host renderer. It is not a claim that the handle can be used again.

    `MarkdownDocument` holds `ProgressiveMarkdownRendering` — a protocol the host's own `ProgressiveMarkdownRender` already satisfies — so `renderProgressiveOrHost` returns either implementation and the document never learns which. The gates are that the pieces JOIN UP to the whole-document render byte for byte, and that block ids keep counting across pieces rather than restarting per chunk; both are checked at the FFI door and again from Swift.

    Every FFI loop test carries an iteration guard. One did not, and a mutation that stopped `_next` from advancing hung the run — which skipped the restore step and left the mutated file in the tree. That is the failure mode invariant 30's discipline exists for, re-earned here: a bounded loop is not tidiness, it is what makes the cleanup reachable.

132. **⌘P must not photograph the window, and the fixture that proves it must actually WRAP.** Invariant 127 fixed `--pdf` by seeding its off-screen window to the paper column; the interactive path was still handing AppKit the window's own layout and letting `.fit` scale it onto the sheet, so the same document printed differently depending on how wide the reader happened to have the window. The unpaged branch of `makePrintOperation` now borrows the paper column — `.clip` plus `beginUnpagedPrintColumn(paperWidth:left:right:)` — and gives it back in `printDidRun`. Giving it back is not housekeeping: the print panel is modal, so a reader never sees the paper-width layout unless it is left behind, and then every reading-position offset moves under them.

    **The page-count check passed with the fix reverted, and the reason was the fixture.** Its paragraphs were one line long, so the text laid out IDENTICALLY at a 619pt container and a 1519pt one — the same used height to twelve decimal places — and the window's width could not change the page count whatever the print path did. Nothing about the harness was wrong; it varied the column correctly. A test that measures a width-dependent quantity needs content whose height actually depends on the width, and "long enough to wrap on any column" in a comment is not evidence that it does.

133. **Pooling the paragraph format took the host's decode from 463.3 ms to 368.7 ms, and the walk had to go into BOTH assemblers — the exporter alone left every real document disagreeing with itself.** P4b pooled pictures and per-edge borders and parked `format`, on an estimate. Measured on the same 2,562-page government manual, `format` is the biggest of the three by the measure that matters: **10,864 occurrences of 1,635 distinct values**, 1,655,374 bytes. That is TWICE `edge_borders`' occurrence count (5,494) for slightly FEWER bytes — and invariant 129 established the host's bill is the nested container Foundation opens per occurrence, not the bytes inside it. Ranking the three by size would have put this last; ranking them by what the host pays puts it first.

    Two halves, and only one needed a pool. A format that says nothing now leaves the wire entirely (`ParagraphFormat::is_default` on the field) instead of writing `"format":{"contextual_spacing":false}` per block — that removes a container rather than shrinking one. A format that says something is filed in `paragraph_format_pool` and replaced by a `format_ref` slot, which is a SIBLING key on the paragraph rather than a field inside `format`: a slot nested one level down would have re-opened the very container the repair exists to close.

    **Measured, paired — one host binary, two payloads, alternating in one session.** The pre-change payload came from a temporary worktree at `a7298cb`; the current host decodes it unchanged, because an inline format still wins over a slot. Best of three, discarding the first round as cold start:

    | payload | bytes | `RustEngine.decodeOffice` |
    |---|---|---|
    | before | 11,516,320 | **463.3 ms** |
    | after | 10,236,263 | **368.7 ms** |

    Both decode to 3,207 blocks. **−94.6 ms, −20.4%** — real, and BELOW the roadmap's −123 ms estimate, which is the third time in this sprint an estimate about this wire has missed. Estimate the direction, measure the size.

    **The defect this earned: there are TWO assemblers, and pooling in one is worse than pooling in neither.** `office_export::to_json` and `office_project::project` both write this wire, and every real document comes through the projection one. Wiring the interner into the exporter alone made the two disagree on every paragraph in the corpus — caught by `office_projection_oracle`'s real-document check, which reported `format` present only on one side and `format_ref` only on the other. `the_projection_assembler_pools_repeated_paragraph_formats` and `both_assemblers_agree_on_a_document_with_repeated_paragraph_formats` now name that case directly instead of leaving it to a real file to notice.

    **A partial walk cannot be told from a complete one by its output.** After the first wiring, 409 formats were still inline. Widening the interner to master-page and anchored objects — whose text boxes hold ordinary blocks — recovered 21 of them, and dumping the payload and counting the remaining `format` keys by path showed the other 388 are `TableFormat`, a different type the paragraph walk was never meant to reach. Counting by PATH rather than by total is what settled it; the total alone would have left "did the walk miss something" unanswerable.

    Mutation-checked three ways, each restored and verified: every slot pointing at entry 0 fails the unit test that says a use points at the RIGHT entry; the projection assembler dropping its pool fails four oracle checks including both real documents; and the host's `resolve` always answering with the default fails the host table's lookups AND its out-of-range throw.

134. **A provenance claim that parses is not a claim that aims, and the one broken marker everyone could see was hiding four stale ones nobody could.** `port-coverage.py` reported exactly one MALFORMED claim for a long time — `// swift: Render/Office/OfficeBlock.swift:231a`, unparseable because `231a` is not a line number. It was not a typo. It was a marker meaning "the gradient field was inserted here and I have not renumbered what follows", and what followed was stale by exactly the five lines `backgroundGradient` added: the border colour/width claim said 232-238 for code at 237-245, `edgeBorders` said 241-244 for 246-249, `width` said 245-251 for 250-256.

    **Fixing the visible one alone would have made the invisible ones invisible for good.** So the check was widened first: a claim is now also read against the Swift file it NAMES, and one that runs past the end of that file, or names a file that is not there, is AIMLESS — reported beside MALFORMED and failing the same gate. Both are credited to nothing while looking exactly like a claim that works.

    **The first version of that check was wrong, and the corpus said so.** It also rejected a claim landing on nothing but blank lines, and named 35 — every one correct by design. Each module ends with a block of them under a comment that says what they are: "Boundary lines (closing braces, blank separators, field/case lines already covered in substance by the ranges above) that the coverage script's per-item markers did not individually re-state". Claiming a blank separator is how the denominator gets accounted for rather than quietly missed; flagging it would have told the port to stop doing the one thing that keeps its own number honest. The rule was dropped before anything was "fixed" on the strength of it.

    The three real findings the corrected check kept are not claims at all — they are SENTENCES whose first words collide with the claim syntax (`// swift: DocxReader.swift:463-465 binds the original 'numbering' with '_' and omits...`).
